What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Narrow an ambiguous incident report and agree on an evidence-based update.
  • Challenge premature certainty and agree on comparisons that separate hypotheses.
  • Clarify the required task and refuse excessive access while offering a legitimate route.
  • Compare incomplete estimates and qualify a recommendation without inventing missing costs.
  • Report deployment states accurately and define evidence needed for completion.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

01IT Service Language, Tickets, and Triage"The system is down" means the export fails+

Your goal

Narrow an ambiguous incident report and agree on an evidence-based update.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

"The system is down" means the export fails

Ren reports that the finance system is down. Ren can sign in and view invoices, but exporting the current month produces an error. Two colleagues report the same export problem; other users and functions have not been checked. The billing team needs its file by 3 p.m. Asha owns service-desk triage and can escalate the ticket, but cannot promise a repair time.

Asha: Service-desk analyst / Ren: Billing coordinator

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which function is confirmed to fail?

Answer and explanation

D. Exporting the current month's invoices

The briefing confirms an export error while sign-in and invoice viewing still work for Ren.

2. How many people have reported the export issue?

Answer and explanation

D. Three

Ren and two colleagues have reported it; the wider affected population is unknown.

3. What may Asha promise?

Answer and explanation

A. An escalation and a timed update

Asha can coordinate triage and escalation, but the briefing gives no confirmed repair time or authority over billing.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Urgency concerns time pressure; impact concerns consequences and scope. Priority normally reflects an organization's agreed assessment, not just the strongest wording in a ticket. A response commitment is not a repair guarantee.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
service desk
The contact point coordinating user support.contact the service desk
ticket
A recorded request or issue for tracking.update the ticket
triage
Initial assessment of impact, priority, and ownership.triage an incident
incident
An unplanned disruption or reduction in service.report a service incident
service request
A request for a routine agreed service.fulfill a service request
impact
The effect on users or business work.assess business impact
urgency
How quickly action is needed in the circumstances.establish the urgency
priority
The relative order or importance assigned to work.assign a ticket priority
affected user
A person experiencing the reported problem.identify affected users
scope
The functions, users, or locations included.establish the incident scope
symptom
An observed sign of a problem.record the exact symptom
error message
Text identifying or describing an error condition.capture the error message
reproduce
Make the reported behavior occur again.reproduce the export failure
workaround
A temporary way to reduce impact without fixing the cause.verify a workaround
resolution
Restoration or completion according to the agreed criteria.confirm the resolution
response time
Time until support responds under a defined measure.distinguish response time from repair time
restoration time
Time until a disrupted service is restored.estimate restoration time cautiously
escalation
Referral for higher authority or specialist attention.request a technical escalation
assignment group
The team designated to handle a ticket.confirm the assignment group
service owner
The role accountable for the service.notify the service owner
knowledge article
A maintained support reference for a known task or issue.check the knowledge article
duplicate ticket
Another ticket reporting the same underlying incident.link a duplicate ticket
status update
A communication of progress and remaining uncertainty.send a timed status update
closure criterion
A condition required before work is marked closed.agree on closure criteria

A workaround may reduce impact while the cause remains unresolved. Closing a duplicate ticket does not close the underlying incident. State where the authoritative progress record remains available.

C Notice the language

Clarification is part of taking the problem seriously

A user may describe the whole system as down because one blocked function prevents their work. Acknowledge the business impact, then narrow the technical description without arguing about the word down.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge impact
I understand the export is blocking your billing file.
Narrow the function
Which action fails after you sign in?
Check what works
Can you still view the invoices on screen?
Establish scope
Who else has tried the same export and seen the error?
Request evidence
Please send the exact error text through the approved support channel.
Separate deadlines
Your billing deadline is 3 p.m.; the repair time is not yet known.
Offer a commitment
I will escalate the ticket and update you by noon.
Read back the issue
Sign-in and viewing work; the current-month export fails for three reported users.
Check a workaround
Has anyone verified that the alternative produces the required file?
Avoid a promise
I cannot confirm a restoration time before the technical assessment.
Explain escalation
I am asking the application team to investigate this function.
Preserve uncertainty
Other functions and users have not yet been checked.
Link related reports
I will link the reports so the investigation has one shared record.
Confirm the owner
Which team is accepting the next investigation step?
Define closure
We will confirm that the required export works before closing the incident.
Close the update
The next message will include findings, outstanding questions, and the next owner.
6 language and precision notes
Can versus cannot
Paired statements such as "can sign in but cannot export" describe the boundary of failure.
So far
"Three users so far" preserves the possibility that the scope is wider.
By versus at
"By noon" sets a latest time; "at noon" names a specific time.
Reported versus affected
Three reported users does not prove that only three users are affected.
Will update
A promise to update names a controllable communication action, not an unverified repair outcome.
Present perfect
"Who has tried the export?" asks about attempts relevant to the current investigation.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which ticket summary is most accurate?

Answer and explanation

C. Current-month export fails for three reported users; sign-in and viewing work for Ren.

The accurate summary preserves confirmed functions and reported scope without extending the evidence.

2. Which statement promises communication rather than repair?

Answer and explanation

D. I will send a progress update by noon.

An update can report incomplete findings; it does not guarantee that service will be restored.

3. Which question establishes business impact?

Answer and explanation

D. What work cannot proceed without that file?

The blocked task establishes business consequences. Error codes, comparison periods and ownership are useful triage details but answer different questions.

4. What does "three reported users" avoid claiming?

Answer and explanation

D. That the affected population is limited to three

Reported counts identify known reports, not a verified upper limit on all affected users.

Check explanations in the book, page 85 ↗

E Conversations

"The system is down" means the export fails

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Asha: Service-desk analyst / Ren: Billing coordinator

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • impact
  • error message
  • assignment group
  • escalation
  • restoration time
  • workaround
  • symptom
  • closure criterion
  • scope
  • duplicate ticket
  1. Ren
    The finance system is down, and billing needs the monthly file by three. I have already tried twice, so this is becoming urgent for us.
  2. Asha
    I understand the business 1. To route this correctly, can you tell me which action fails after you open the application?
  3. Ren
    I can sign in and see the invoices. When I choose the current month and export, I get an error instead of the file.
  4. Asha
    That narrows the 2. I will record an export failure, while keeping the billing deadline visible in the ticket.
  5. Ren
    Two colleagues have the same problem. We have not checked older periods or asked the other office. Does that mean it is a major outage?
  6. Asha
    It means three users have reported this 3. I need the agreed impact assessment before assigning a broader classification, but I can escalate now.
  7. Ren
    Please do. Would a screenshot help? It shows invoice details, so I do not want to paste it into the general team chat.
  8. Asha
    Use the approved support channel and follow its handling rules. The exact 4 and approximate time will help the application team locate the event.
  9. Ren
    Someone suggested copying the visible rows manually. I am worried that might omit invoices across multiple pages, and we have not checked the totals.
  10. Asha
    Then it is an unverified 5, not a solution I can recommend as complete. I will ask the service owner to assess an acceptable alternative.
  11. Ren
    Can I tell my manager the technical team will fix it before three? They are asking me for a firm answer about the billing run.
  12. Asha
    I cannot promise a 6 before their assessment. I can commit to a progress update by noon, even if the cause remains unknown.
  13. Ren
    Noon for an update, not necessarily a fix. I'll make that clear to my manager. Please put it in the ticket too.
  14. Asha
    I will. I am sending a technical 7 with the confirmed scope, the error details, and your business deadline as separate fields.
  15. Ren
    My colleagues each opened a ticket. Should they close theirs now, or leave them open so the team can see that this is not just me?
  16. Asha
    I will link them to the main record as related reports. A 8 can be managed without suggesting that the underlying export problem is resolved.
  17. Ren
    Could you read that back before you send it? I don't want the team checking invoice viewing when it's the export I need.
  18. Asha
    Certainly. Sign-in and viewing work for you; current-month export fails for three reported users. Other functions remain unchecked. Billing needs the file by three, and the 9 is the application support team.
  19. Ren
    That captures it. Please include me on the noon update. Once they say it is fixed, I can verify the export and compare the file totals.
  20. Asha
    I will record that as the 10. We will distinguish a reported fix from your confirmation that the required task works again.

Answers and explanations
  1. impact Turn 2. Impact describes the work disrupted, here preparation of the billing file, rather than merely the technical error.
  2. scope Turn 4. Scope identifies the affected function and population; the current evidence points to export rather than complete application unavailability.
  3. symptom Turn 6. A symptom is the observed failure; its extent and underlying cause still need assessment before classifying the whole incident.
  4. error message Turn 8. The error message gives diagnostic wording; it should be recorded without unnecessarily widening access to invoice data.
  5. workaround Turn 10. A workaround reduces impact temporarily; an unverified manual copy may not produce the complete file needed for billing.
  6. restoration time Turn 12. Restoration time concerns when service works again; it is different from a support response or scheduled update.
  7. escalation Turn 14. Escalation brings the application specialists into the investigation; it does not itself establish a repair deadline.
  8. duplicate ticket Turn 16. A duplicate ticket may refer to the same underlying incident; linking it preserves evidence without treating it as a separate resolved fault.
  9. assignment group Turn 18. The assignment group identifies the team handling the next investigation step rather than the business user awaiting the result.
  10. closure criterion Turn 20. A closure criterion states the evidence needed before marking the incident resolved, here a working export with the required file checks.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Ren: The finance system is down, and billing needs the monthly file by three. I have already tried twice, so this is becoming urgent for us.
  2. Asha: I understand the business impact. To route this correctly, can you tell me which action fails after you open the application?
  3. Ren: I can sign in and see the invoices. When I choose the current month and export, I get an error instead of the file.
  4. Asha: That narrows the scope. I will record an export failure, while keeping the billing deadline visible in the ticket.
  5. Ren: Two colleagues have the same problem. We have not checked older periods or asked the other office. Does that mean it is a major outage?
  6. Asha: It means three users have reported this symptom. I need the agreed impact assessment before assigning a broader classification, but I can escalate now.
  7. Ren: Please do. Would a screenshot help? It shows invoice details, so I do not want to paste it into the general team chat.
  8. Asha: Use the approved support channel and follow its handling rules. The exact error message and approximate time will help the application team locate the event.
  9. Ren: Someone suggested copying the visible rows manually. I am worried that might omit invoices across multiple pages, and we have not checked the totals.
  10. Asha: Then it is an unverified workaround, not a solution I can recommend as complete. I will ask the service owner to assess an acceptable alternative.
  11. Ren: Can I tell my manager the technical team will fix it before three? They are asking me for a firm answer about the billing run.
  12. Asha: I cannot promise a restoration time before their assessment. I can commit to a progress update by noon, even if the cause remains unknown.
  13. Ren: Noon for an update, not necessarily a fix. I'll make that clear to my manager. Please put it in the ticket too.
  14. Asha: I will. I am sending a technical escalation with the confirmed scope, the error details, and your business deadline as separate fields.
  15. Ren: My colleagues each opened a ticket. Should they close theirs now, or leave them open so the team can see that this is not just me?
  16. Asha: I will link them to the main record as related reports. A duplicate ticket can be managed without suggesting that the underlying export problem is resolved.
  17. Ren: Could you read that back before you send it? I don't want the team checking invoice viewing when it's the export I need.
  18. Asha: Certainly. Sign-in and viewing work for you; current-month export fails for three reported users. Other functions remain unchecked. Billing needs the file by three, and the assignment group is the application support team.
  19. Ren: That captures it. Please include me on the noon update. Once they say it is fixed, I can verify the export and compare the file totals.
  20. Asha: I will record that as the closure criterion. We will distinguish a reported fix from your confirmation that the required task works again.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Narrowing the invoice-export incident

The service desk clarifies the lesson's ticket: sign-in works, invoice export does not, and other functions are untested.

  1. Service desk analyst: Your ticket says the system is down. Could you describe the action that fails?
  2. Billing specialist: I can sign in, but I cannot export invoices from the application.
  3. Service desk analyst: Thank you. Have you checked any other functions, or only sign-in and export?
  4. Billing specialist: Only those two. I don't know whether anyone else has the same issue.
  5. Service desk analyst: I'll record an invoice-export incident rather than a confirmed outage of the whole system.
  6. Billing specialist: Is that different from a service request for a new export feature?
  7. Service desk analyst: Yes. You're reporting a function that isn't working, not requesting a new capability.
  8. Billing specialist: Can you tell me when it will be fixed under the SLA?
  9. Service desk analyst: I need to check the applicable service agreement and triage the impact before discussing timing.
  10. Billing specialist: Understood. I'll describe the failed action in the ticket without expanding the affected scope.
A printer queue needs an impact check

A separate fictional office: one receptionist reports a stuck label-printing job; another printer has not been tested.

  1. Service desk analyst: Which printer is affected, and what happens when you submit a label job?
  2. Reception supervisor: The reception label job stays in the queue. I haven't tested another printer.
  3. Service desk analyst: Does the ticket concern a single stuck job or every job in that queue?
  4. Reception supervisor: I only know about my job. I shouldn't have written that printing is unavailable everywhere.
  5. Service desk analyst: That's useful clarification. What work is currently blocked by this incident?
  6. Reception supervisor: I need these labels for reception work, but I haven't assessed the wider impact.
  7. Service desk analyst: I'll capture that limitation and ask the support team to check the queue.
  8. Reception supervisor: Should I raise a separate problem record because this feels familiar?
  9. Service desk analyst: Let's first check earlier tickets. A similar symptom doesn't establish a shared underlying problem.
  10. Reception supervisor: I'll provide the job reference and avoid creating duplicate tickets while you review it.
A request for equipment is not a repair

A separate fictional onboarding case: a hiring coordinator requests a second monitor for a new starter; no existing device has failed.

  1. Hiring coordinator: I marked this urgent incident because the new starter needs a second monitor.
  2. Service desk analyst: Has any assigned equipment failed, or are you requesting additional equipment?
  3. Hiring coordinator: Nothing has failed. This is an additional monitor, and approval hasn't been checked.
  4. Service desk analyst: Then let's describe it as a service request and identify the approval route.
  5. Hiring coordinator: Will changing the category guarantee that the equipment arrives before the start date?
  6. Service desk analyst: No. Categorization clarifies the work; stock, approval, and fulfillment timing still need checking.
  7. Hiring coordinator: What information should I supply so the request can be assessed properly?
  8. Service desk analyst: Please provide the role's requirement and intended start date, without assuming the request is approved.
  9. Hiring coordinator: I'll add those details and ask who can authorize the equipment.
  10. Service desk analyst: I'll confirm the next step and applicable service expectations once the request is reviewed.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

"The system is down" means the export fails
  1. Check the dialogue. Read the confirmed boundary: Ren can sign in and view invoices, but current-month export fails for three reported users.
  2. Switch roles. Keep the 3 p.m. billing deadline separate from the unknown repair time; repeat the noon progress-update commitment.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Printing fails, but document editing still works

A user can edit and save documents but cannot print to one office printer. Another printer has not been tested. The user needs printed copies for a 2 p.m. meeting.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Analyst: "The confirmed ___ is printing to that device, not document editing."

Answer and explanation

B. failure

The user can edit and save; only printing to the named device is confirmed to fail.

2. User: "I have not verified an alternative ___ yet."

Answer and explanation

A. printer

Another printer is untested, so it cannot be presented as a confirmed workaround.

3. Analyst: "I will report the meeting deadline separately from the unknown ___ time."

Answer and explanation

A. restoration

The meeting's deadline does not establish when the printer service will be restored.

4. User: "Please give me a timed ___ even if the repair is still pending."

Answer and explanation

A. update

A progress update is a controllable communication action and can accurately report an unfinished repair.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Another round with an AI partner

Extend the vocabularyPractice the grammarRehearse with AI

Copy a finished AI prompt for this lesson

These prompts are fully written and include this lesson's facts and language. Open one, copy it as written, and paste it into a new AI chat.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 01: IT Service Language, Tickets, and Triage
Original fictional case: Ren reports that the finance system is down. Ren can sign in and view invoices, but exporting the current month produces an error. Two colleagues report the same export problem; other users and functions have not been checked. The billing team needs its file by 3 p.m. Asha owns service-desk triage and can escalate the ticket, but cannot promise a repair time.
Language workshop: "The system is down" means the export fails
Communication goal: Narrow an ambiguous incident report and agree on an evidence-based update.
Language guidance: Urgency concerns time pressure; impact concerns consequences and scope. Priority normally reflects an organization's agreed assessment, not just the strongest wording in a ticket. A response commitment is not a repair guarantee.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I understand the export is blocking your billing file. / Which action fails after you sign in? / Can you still view the invoices on screen? / Who else has tried the same export and seen the error? / Please send the exact error text through the approved support channel. / Your billing deadline is 3 p.m.; the repair time is not yet known. / I will escalate the ticket and update you by noon. / Sign-in and viewing work; the current-month export fails for three reported users. / Has anyone verified that the alternative produces the required file? / I cannot confirm a restoration time before the technical assessment. / I am asking the application team to investigate this function. / Other functions and users have not yet been checked. / I will link the reports so the investigation has one shared record. / Which team is accepting the next investigation step? / We will confirm that the required export works before closing the incident. / The next message will include findings, outstanding questions, and the next owner.
Vocabulary: service desk: The contact point coordinating user support. / ticket: A recorded request or issue for tracking. / triage: Initial assessment of impact, priority, and ownership. / incident: An unplanned disruption or reduction in service. / service request: A request for a routine agreed service. / impact: The effect on users or business work. / urgency: How quickly action is needed in the circumstances. / priority: The relative order or importance assigned to work. / affected user: A person experiencing the reported problem. / scope: The functions, users, or locations included. / symptom: An observed sign of a problem. / error message: Text identifying or describing an error condition. / reproduce: Make the reported behavior occur again. / workaround: A temporary way to reduce impact without fixing the cause. / resolution: Restoration or completion according to the agreed criteria. / response time: Time until support responds under a defined measure. / restoration time: Time until a disrupted service is restored. / escalation: Referral for higher authority or specialist attention. / assignment group: The team designated to handle a ticket. / service owner: The role accountable for the service. / knowledge article: A maintained support reference for a known task or issue. / duplicate ticket: Another ticket reporting the same underlying incident. / status update: A communication of progress and remaining uncertainty. / closure criterion: A condition required before work is marked closed.
Speaking task: Check the dialogue. Read the confirmed boundary: Ren can sign in and view invoices, but current-month export fails for three reported users. Switch roles. Keep the 3 p.m. billing deadline separate from the unknown repair time; repeat the noon progress-update commitment. Complete the printer exchange. Preserve the untested alternative and do not promise restoration before the 2 p.m. meeting.
Partner: Asha: Service-desk analyst / Ren: Billing coordinator
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A user can edit and save documents but cannot print to one office printer. Another printer has not been tested. The user needs printed copies for a 2 p.m. meeting.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Ren: Could you read that back before you send it? I don't want the team checking invoice viewing when it's the export I need.
Asha: Certainly. Sign-in and viewing work for you; current-month export fails for three reported users. Other functions remain unchecked. Billing needs the file by three, and the assignment group is the application support team.
Ren: That captures it. Please include me on the noon update. Once they say it is fixed, I can verify the export and compare the file totals.
Asha: I will record that as the closure criterion. We will distinguish a reported fix from your confirmation that the required task works again.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Lesson 01: IT Service Language, Tickets, and Triage
Original fictional case: Ren reports that the finance system is down. Ren can sign in and view invoices, but exporting the current month produces an error. Two colleagues report the same export problem; other users and functions have not been checked. The billing team needs its file by 3 p.m. Asha owns service-desk triage and can escalate the ticket, but cannot promise a repair time.
Language workshop: "The system is down" means the export fails
Communication goal: Narrow an ambiguous incident report and agree on an evidence-based update.
Language guidance: Urgency concerns time pressure; impact concerns consequences and scope. Priority normally reflects an organization's agreed assessment, not just the strongest wording in a ticket. A response commitment is not a repair guarantee.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I understand the export is blocking your billing file. / Which action fails after you sign in? / Can you still view the invoices on screen? / Who else has tried the same export and seen the error? / Please send the exact error text through the approved support channel. / Your billing deadline is 3 p.m.; the repair time is not yet known. / I will escalate the ticket and update you by noon. / Sign-in and viewing work; the current-month export fails for three reported users. / Has anyone verified that the alternative produces the required file? / I cannot confirm a restoration time before the technical assessment. / I am asking the application team to investigate this function. / Other functions and users have not yet been checked. / I will link the reports so the investigation has one shared record. / Which team is accepting the next investigation step? / We will confirm that the required export works before closing the incident. / The next message will include findings, outstanding questions, and the next owner.
Vocabulary: service desk: The contact point coordinating user support. / ticket: A recorded request or issue for tracking. / triage: Initial assessment of impact, priority, and ownership. / incident: An unplanned disruption or reduction in service. / service request: A request for a routine agreed service. / impact: The effect on users or business work. / urgency: How quickly action is needed in the circumstances. / priority: The relative order or importance assigned to work. / affected user: A person experiencing the reported problem. / scope: The functions, users, or locations included. / symptom: An observed sign of a problem. / error message: Text identifying or describing an error condition. / reproduce: Make the reported behavior occur again. / workaround: A temporary way to reduce impact without fixing the cause. / resolution: Restoration or completion according to the agreed criteria. / response time: Time until support responds under a defined measure. / restoration time: Time until a disrupted service is restored. / escalation: Referral for higher authority or specialist attention. / assignment group: The team designated to handle a ticket. / service owner: The role accountable for the service. / knowledge article: A maintained support reference for a known task or issue. / duplicate ticket: Another ticket reporting the same underlying incident. / status update: A communication of progress and remaining uncertainty. / closure criterion: A condition required before work is marked closed.
Speaking task: Check the dialogue. Read the confirmed boundary: Ren can sign in and view invoices, but current-month export fails for three reported users. Switch roles. Keep the 3 p.m. billing deadline separate from the unknown repair time; repeat the noon progress-update commitment. Complete the printer exchange. Preserve the untested alternative and do not promise restoration before the 2 p.m. meeting.
Partner: Asha: Service-desk analyst / Ren: Billing coordinator
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A user can edit and save documents but cannot print to one office printer. Another printer has not been tested. The user needs printed copies for a 2 p.m. meeting.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Ren: Could you read that back before you send it? I don't want the team checking invoice viewing when it's the export I need.
Asha: Certainly. Sign-in and viewing work for you; current-month export fails for three reported users. Other functions remain unchecked. Billing needs the file by three, and the assignment group is the application support team.
Ren: That captures it. Please include me on the noon update. Once they say it is fixed, I can verify the export and compare the file totals.
Asha: I will record that as the closure criterion. We will distinguish a reported fix from your confirmation that the required task works again.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. 02Networks, Connectivity, and Root-Cause HypothesesThe VPN pattern is a clue, not a DNS diagnosis+

Your goal

Challenge premature certainty and agree on comparisons that separate hypotheses.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

The VPN pattern is a clue, not a DNS diagnosis

The staff portal works from the office network but fails for four people connected through the virtual private network (VPN). The same portal address is used. Nobody has checked name resolution, routing, or access-policy logs for those requests. Eli says DNS must be the cause. Nia coordinates network investigation. The team has an approved diagnostic process and must not change production settings merely to test a guess.

Eli: Support engineer / Nia: Network engineer

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which pattern is established?

Answer and explanation

D. Office access works; four VPN users report failure.

The reports establish an access-path difference, but no specific network mechanism has yet been tested.

2. Which cause is confirmed?

Answer and explanation

A. None of the proposed causes

The briefing explicitly says the relevant checks have not been performed.

3. What constraint applies to investigation?

Answer and explanation

C. Follow the approved process without changing production on a guess.

The case permits approved diagnosis, not speculative production changes or unsupported status claims.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A timeout, a refused connection, and a certificate warning are different observations. "The VPN is connected" does not establish that every destination is reachable. Name the source, destination, and observed result.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
VPN
Virtual private network; a connection providing a defined private access path.connect through the VPN
DNS
Domain Name System; the system that resolves names to records.investigate a DNS hypothesis
name resolution
Translating a name into the records needed for a connection.verify name resolution
IP address
A numerical network address used by the Internet Protocol.compare resolved IP addresses
hostname
A name identifying a host or service endpoint.confirm the hostname
resolver
A service that answers or obtains DNS responses.identify the configured resolver
cache
Stored data reused to avoid repeating work.inspect a cached response
TTL
Time to live; a lifetime limit, with meaning depending on context.check a DNS record's TTL
routing
Selection of paths for network traffic.review routing evidence
route
A path or forwarding rule for reaching a destination.inspect the relevant route
gateway
A system providing a path between networks or services.identify the gateway
subnet
A defined subdivision of an IP network.compare affected subnets
firewall
A control that permits or blocks network traffic under rules.review firewall logs
access policy
Rules determining permitted access under stated conditions.check the access policy
port
A numbered transport endpoint associated with a service.identify the service port
packet loss
Failure of transmitted network packets to reach their destination.measure packet loss
round-trip time
Time for a request and return response across a path.compare round-trip times
timeout
Expiry of a waiting period before a response completes.record a connection timeout
connection refusal
An explicit rejection of a connection attempt.distinguish refusal from timeout
TLS
Transport Layer Security; protection for communication in transit.inspect a TLS handshake error
certificate
A signed digital statement used to bind identity and a public key.review a certificate warning
proxy
An intermediary handling requests between client and destination.identify the proxy path
reachability
Whether a destination can be contacted under specified conditions.test service reachability
diagnostic hypothesis
A proposed explanation that still requires testing.test a diagnostic hypothesis

DNS, routing, and access policy are different parts of connectivity. A working office path can narrow the comparison but does not by itself identify which VPN-path component differs. TTL has protocol-specific meanings.

C Notice the language

Question the inference, not the colleague

A confident diagnosis can spread faster than its evidence. Acknowledge the useful pattern and ask what would distinguish competing explanations. "What supports DNS over routing?" preserves the investigation without making disagreement personal.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge a pattern
The reports do point to a difference between access paths.
Challenge certainty
What evidence establishes DNS rather than another path issue?
State a hypothesis
Name resolution is one explanation we still need to test.
Request exact wording
Was it a timeout, a refusal, or a certificate warning?
Define a comparison
Compare the same destination from the two approved paths.
Preserve controls
We should not change production settings to test an unverified guess.
Name missing evidence
We have not checked the resolver or policy logs for those requests.
Close the inference
The pattern narrows the investigation; it does not yet establish a cause.
Check the source
Were all four reports from the same VPN profile?
Distinguish layers
A successful name lookup does not prove application access.
Limit an observation
The portal worked for this office test at this time.
Avoid a universal claim
We have not tested every VPN user or destination.
Ask for timestamps
Please include the time and time zone for each failed request.
Assign a test
Who will collect the approved comparison and record the results?
Report a negative result
That check did not support the DNS hypothesis.
Preserve the record
Keep the observed results separate from the proposed explanation.
6 language and precision notes
Must versus may
"DNS must be the cause" expresses certainty; "DNS may be involved" presents a possibility.
Rather than
Use "what supports DNS rather than routing?" to compare explanations without claiming either is established.
From versus through
"From the office" identifies the source; "through the VPN" identifies a path.
Did not support
A negative check weakens a particular explanation without necessarily proving every alternative.
Which error
Asking for the exact error prevents different symptoms from being collapsed into one label.
At this time
Time qualifications matter because intermittent faults can change between checks.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which statement preserves the evidence boundary?

Answer and explanation

C. The VPN path is associated with the reported failures; the cause is unconfirmed.

The observed path difference is real, but the specific cause still requires testing.

2. Which question distinguishes the observed failure from a proposed cause?

Answer and explanation

D. Did the request time out, get refused, or show a certificate warning?

Asking for the actual observed outcome preserves symptoms. The alternatives assume mechanisms or causes that have not been tested.

3. What does a successful name lookup establish?

Answer and explanation

B. That this lookup returned a result under the tested conditions

Name resolution is one step and cannot certify later connectivity or authorization.

4. Which commitment is appropriate before diagnosis?

Answer and explanation

A. I will report the comparison results and remaining hypotheses.

A result-based update is achievable without promising an unverified repair or bypassing controls.

  18. Nia

  19. Eli

Answers and explanations
  1. hypothesis Turn 2. A hypothesis is a proposed explanation; the path difference alone does not establish which network component caused it.
  2. symptom Turn 4. The symptom is the observed failure, such as a timeout or warning, not the user's general description that the page will not open.
  3. reachability Turn 6. Reachability concerns contacting the particular destination under the tested conditions; VPN connection status alone does not establish it.
  4. name resolution Turn 8. Name resolution maps the portal name to records; comparing results helps assess the DNS hypothesis without assuming it is correct.
  5. hostname Turn 10. The hostname identifies the specific named destination; a comparable investigation needs the same target rather than merely the same application description.
  6. access policy Turn 12. An access policy governs permission under specified conditions; it is another possible explanation that requires its own evidence.
  7. timeout Turn 14. A timeout means the waiting period expired; it differs from a completed but slow response or an explicit refusal.
  8. time zone Turn 16. The time zone makes timestamps comparable across logs and reports; a clock time alone can point investigators to the wrong events.
  9. comparison Turn 18. A comparison uses specified, comparable conditions; it can narrow explanations without turning an untested alternative into a finding.
  10. diagnosis Turn 20. A diagnosis is an evidence-based explanation; revising an early theory is part of reaching it rather than a failure of the investigation.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The VPN difference is not a DNS diagnosis

Engineers examine the lesson's internal application, reachable in the office but not through the VPN.

  1. Service desk lead: People can reach the application in the office, but not through the VPN.
  2. Network engineer: That comparison is useful. It doesn't yet establish whether DNS is responsible.
  3. Service desk lead: A colleague already announced a DNS fault. Should I repeat that in the update?
  4. Network engineer: No. Say the failure is observed on the VPN path and the cause is unconfirmed.
  5. Service desk lead: Could a gateway or firewall issue also fit the symptom we have?
  6. Network engineer: Those are possible hypotheses, not findings. We need evidence from the affected connection.
  7. Service desk lead: What comparison would help without changing several settings at the same time?
  8. Network engineer: Check name resolution and connection results for the same application from both paths.
  9. Service desk lead: I'll preserve the observed difference and remove the unsupported diagnosis from my summary.
  10. Network engineer: I'll investigate the hypotheses and report which tests support or weaken each one.
One hostname works, another does not

A separate fictional network test: a laptop reaches the intranet hostname but not a reporting hostname; no DNS response has been inspected.

  1. Network technician: The intranet opens, but the reporting hostname doesn't. I suspect a name-resolution issue.
  2. Application engineer: That's possible, but have we inspected the DNS response for the reporting name?
  3. Network technician: Not yet. I only have the browser result from this laptop.
  4. Application engineer: Then don't describe a DNS failure as confirmed. The browser result covers several possible stages.
  5. Network technician: Would a successful intranet visit rule out the gateway as a cause?
  6. Application engineer: It shows one path worked, not that every destination uses an identical path.
  7. Network technician: I'll collect the reporting lookup result and the time of the failed attempt.
  8. Application engineer: Good. Keep the application name and test device consistent so the evidence is comparable.
  9. Network technician: We can then decide whether to investigate resolution, routing, or the application response.
  10. Application engineer: Exactly. The update should distinguish our observation from the explanation we're still testing.
A firewall change and an uncertain cause

A separate fictional incident: connection errors appeared after a firewall change, but no rule-level evidence has been reviewed.

  1. Incident coordinator: Connection errors began after the firewall change. Can we say the change caused them?
  2. Network engineer: We can report the timing, but we haven't linked the errors to a particular rule.
  3. Incident coordinator: The sequence looks convincing. What evidence would make the causal statement stronger?
  4. Network engineer: We need affected connection details and relevant firewall logs, compared with the approved change.
  5. Incident coordinator: Should I ask users to bypass the firewall while we investigate?
  6. Network engineer: No. We should use the authorized incident process, not invent an unreviewed workaround.
  7. Incident coordinator: Then I'll say the change is a hypothesis under investigation, not the established root cause.
  8. Network engineer: Please include the observed error period and avoid claiming every connection is affected.
  9. Incident coordinator: I'll request the evidence through the incident channel and keep the scope bounded.
  10. Network engineer: I'll review it and explain what we can actually conclude before recommending any change.

Lesson 02: Networks, Connectivity, and Root-Cause Hypotheses
Original fictional case: The staff portal works from the office network but fails for four people connected through the virtual private network (VPN). The same portal address is used. Nobody has checked name resolution, routing, or access-policy logs for those requests. Eli says DNS must be the cause. Nia coordinates network investigation. The team has an approved diagnostic process and must not change production settings merely to test a guess.
Language workshop: The VPN pattern is a clue, not a DNS diagnosis
Communication goal: Challenge premature certainty and agree on comparisons that separate hypotheses.
Language guidance: A timeout, a refused connection, and a certificate warning are different observations. "The VPN is connected" does not establish that every destination is reachable. Name the source, destination, and observed result.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The reports do point to a difference between access paths. / What evidence establishes DNS rather than another path issue? / Name resolution is one explanation we still need to test. / Was it a timeout, a refusal, or a certificate warning? / Compare the same destination from the two approved paths. / We should not change production settings to test an unverified guess. / We have not checked the resolver or policy logs for those requests. / The pattern narrows the investigation; it does not yet establish a cause. / Were all four reports from the same VPN profile? / A successful name lookup does not prove application access. / The portal worked for this office test at this time. / We have not tested every VPN user or destination. / Please include the time and time zone for each failed request. / Who will collect the approved comparison and record the results? / That check did not support the DNS hypothesis. / Keep the observed results separate from the proposed explanation.
Vocabulary: VPN: Virtual private network; a connection providing a defined private access path. / DNS: Domain Name System; the system that resolves names to records. / name resolution: Translating a name into the records needed for a connection. / IP address: A numerical network address used by the Internet Protocol. / hostname: A name identifying a host or service endpoint. / resolver: A service that answers or obtains DNS responses. / cache: Stored data reused to avoid repeating work. / TTL: Time to live; a lifetime limit, with meaning depending on context. / routing: Selection of paths for network traffic. / route: A path or forwarding rule for reaching a destination. / gateway: A system providing a path between networks or services. / subnet: A defined subdivision of an IP network. / firewall: A control that permits or blocks network traffic under rules. / access policy: Rules determining permitted access under stated conditions. / port: A numbered transport endpoint associated with a service. / packet loss: Failure of transmitted network packets to reach their destination. / round-trip time: Time for a request and return response across a path. / timeout: Expiry of a waiting period before a response completes. / connection refusal: An explicit rejection of a connection attempt. / TLS: Transport Layer Security; protection for communication in transit. / certificate: A signed digital statement used to bind identity and a public key. / proxy: An intermediary handling requests between client and destination. / reachability: Whether a destination can be contacted under specified conditions. / diagnostic hypothesis: A proposed explanation that still requires testing.
Speaking task: Check the dialogue. Contrast the working office path with four reported VPN failures; keep DNS as a hypothesis, not a finding. Switch roles. Request the exact warning, path and timestamp through the approved process, without proposing a speculative production change. Complete the certificate exchange. Retain the unreviewed proxy hypothesis and the instruction not to bypass the warning.

Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.

Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.

03Identity, Access, and PermissionsA report request does not justify administrator access+

Your goal

Clarify the required task and refuse excessive access while offering a legitimate route.

This lesson in the book, page 22 ↗

A Read the situation

A report request does not justify administrator access

A temporary analyst, Jo, needs to download a monthly report. Jo requests administrator access because the current account shows an access-denied message. A reporting role may allow the required download, but its exact permissions have not been verified. Imani handles access requests. The manager can confirm Jo's business need; the system owner approves the role. The assignment ends on 30 November.

Jo: Temporary analyst / Imani: Identity and access analyst

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What task does Jo need to perform?

Answer and explanation

C. Download a monthly report

The stated need is a report download, not general administrative control.

2. Who approves the system role?

Answer and explanation

A. The system owner

The manager confirms business need, while the system owner has role-approval authority in this case.

3. What remains unverified?

Answer and explanation

D. Whether the reporting role permits the download

The reporting role is a possible fit, not yet a verified authorization solution.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Authentication establishes identity evidence; authorization governs permitted actions. Being able to sign in does not imply permission to download every report. A role name alone does not prove its actual rights.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
identity
The representation of a user or service in a system.verify the account identity
authentication
Establishing that a claimant controls the required identity evidence.complete authentication
authorization
Permission to perform an action on a resource.check authorization
permission
An allowed action or access right.grant a specific permission
role
A defined collection of access rights or responsibilities.assign a reporting role
RBAC
Role-based access control; access organized through defined roles.review an RBAC assignment
least privilege
Access limited to what is needed for the task.apply least privilege
administrator
A role with broad system-management permissions.justify administrator access
privileged access
Access allowing sensitive or powerful actions.review privileged access
entitlement
A specific granted right to use a resource or capability.review account entitlements
provisioning
Creating or assigning accounts and access.complete approved provisioning
deprovisioning
Removing accounts or access no longer required.schedule deprovisioning
access review
A check that granted access remains appropriate.conduct an access review
access request
A recorded request for specified permissions.submit a scoped access request
business justification
The work-related reason supporting a request.document the business justification
approver
The person authorized to approve a request.identify the correct approver
expiration
The point at which an entitlement or credential ends.set an access expiration
temporary access
Access granted for a defined limited period.request temporary access
separation of duties
Dividing incompatible responsibilities to reduce risk.preserve separation of duties
SSO
Single sign-on; using one authentication session across services.troubleshoot an SSO session
MFA
Multifactor authentication; use of distinct factor types.complete MFA verification
service account
An identity used by an application or automated process.identify the service-account owner
audit log
A record of relevant actions for review.inspect the access audit log
access denied
A message indicating that an attempted action was not permitted.report an access-denied message

Business justification explains why access is needed. Approval establishes permission under the stated process. Temporary access needs a defined end or review condition; it should not silently become permanent.

C Notice the language

Offer a route, not just a refusal

A user may ask for administrator rights as a shortcut because they do not know which role fits the task. Clarify the action, explain the authority boundary, and name the narrower request and approval needed. Urgency does not establish permission.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Clarify the action
Which report do you need, and what action must you perform?
Separate identity and access
You can sign in; the download permission is a different question.
Refuse proportionately
I cannot justify administrator rights from the stated reporting task.
Offer a narrower route
Let us verify whether the reporting role covers that download.
Name the approver
Your manager confirms the need; the system owner approves the role.
Limit duration
The requested access should match the assignment ending on 30 November.
Preserve uncertainty
The role may fit, but its permissions still need checking.
Confirm the request
I will submit the task, resource, duration, and approval route together.
Avoid credential sharing
Please use your own approved account rather than another person's credentials.
Check existing rights
Which entitlement is currently assigned to your account?
Explain least privilege
The request should provide the required action without unrelated control.
Handle urgency
I can flag the deadline without bypassing the approval process.
Check completion
After provisioning, confirm the authorized report action works.
Record an end
Who will verify removal when the assignment finishes?
Distinguish roles
A reporting role and an administrator role serve different purposes.
Close clearly
Approval is pending; the request has been submitted, not granted.
6 language and precision notes
May versus does
"The role may allow download" signals an unverified fit; "the role allows download" claims a confirmed permission.
Need to versus need rights to
Ask for the required task before adopting the user's proposed permission level.
Not yet granted
This states pending authorization without implying permanent refusal.
Until
"Until 30 November" sets an end boundary; specify local handling of the date in the real request.
Can versus authorized to
Technical capability is not the same as organizational permission.
Role of because
Give the concrete reason: "Administrator access is broader than this task requires."

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which request is best scoped?

Answer and explanation

D. Access to download the named monthly report for the assignment period

The correct request states a resource, action, and duration without adding unrelated permissions.

2. Which statement distinguishes authentication from authorization?

Answer and explanation

B. Signing in does not prove permission to download the report.

Identity verification and permission to perform a specific action are separate controls.

3. Who should confirm business need in this case?

Answer and explanation

D. Jo's manager

The case assigns business-need confirmation to Jo's manager; role approval remains the system owner's separate decision.

4. Which status is accurate before approval?

Answer and explanation

B. Submitted for review; access has not been granted.

Submission starts review; it does not establish approval, provisioning, or permanent entitlement.

Check explanations in the book, page 89 ↗

E Conversations

A report request does not justify administrator access

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Jo: Temporary analyst / Imani: Identity and access analyst

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • entitlements
  • Authentication
  • business justification
  • least privilege
  • approver
  • deprovisioning
  • expiration
  • permission
  • provisioning
  • credentials
  1. Jo
    I need administrator access to finish the monthly reporting work. I can sign in, but the download gives me an access-denied message every time.
  2. Imani
    Let us identify the required 1 first. Which report are you downloading, and do you need to change anything in the system?
  3. Jo
    It is the monthly sales summary. I only need to download it and analyze the file. I am not changing accounts or system settings.
  4. Imani
    Then administrator rights would be broader than that 2. There may be a reporting role that provides the specific action.
  5. Jo
    I assumed signing in meant I already had access and the download error was just a technical fault. Are those separate parts of the process?
  6. Imani
    Yes. 3 gets you through the identity check. Authorization determines which resources and actions the account is allowed to use afterward.
  7. Jo
    Could you assign the reporting role now and let me check whether it works? My manager needs the analysis, and I am only here temporarily.
  8. Imani
    The system owner must approve it first. I also need to verify its actual 4, because the role name alone does not confirm that download is included.
  9. Jo
    My manager can confirm the work is required. Does that approval cover the role too, or does the system owner need a separate request?
  10. Imani
    In this process they have different responsibilities. The manager confirms the need, and the system owner is the 5 for the system role.
  11. Jo
    That is clear. The assignment ends on 30 November. I do not expect to need the system afterward unless the contract is formally extended.
  12. Imani
    I will include an 6 aligned with that assignment and the required review route if it changes.
  13. Jo
    While we wait, a colleague offered to download the file using their account. Is that the same as sharing their password so I can do it myself?
  14. Imani
    Those are different proposals, and neither should bypass the approved process. Do not share 7. Ask the report owner whether an authorized delivery route is permitted.
  15. Jo
    I'll ask the report owner. In the meantime, can you flag the deadline and get the role review moving?
  16. Imani
    Exactly. We can expedite review while preserving 8. I will submit the report name, required action, duration, and business justification together.
  17. Jo
    Will the approval email mean I can download immediately, or is there another step after that?
  18. Imani
    Approval and 9 are separate states. After the role is assigned, confirm that the authorized download works with your own account.
  19. Jo
    And at the end of November, the access should be removed unless an extension is reviewed. Please include who checks that so it is not forgotten.
  20. Imani
    I will record 10 ownership. For now the status is submitted for review, not granted, and we have a legitimate route that matches the work you actually need to do.

Answers and explanations
  1. permission Turn 2. A permission authorizes a particular action; the required download does not itself justify broad administrative control.
  2. business justification Turn 4. The business justification states the work-related need, here report analysis, rather than adopting the requested privilege level without examination.
  3. Authentication Turn 6. Authentication establishes identity evidence; it does not automatically authorize every action within the application.
  4. entitlements Turn 8. Entitlements are the specific granted rights attached to the role or account; a helpful role name does not prove the necessary download right.
  5. approver Turn 10. The approver is the person authorized to make this access decision; confirming a business need is not the same decision.
  6. expiration Turn 12. Expiration specifies when the entitlement should end, matching temporary access to the stated assignment rather than leaving it open indefinitely.
  7. credentials Turn 14. Credentials establish access to an identity; sharing them can obscure who acted and does not authorize the recipient's use.
  8. least privilege Turn 16. Least privilege limits access to the legitimate task; urgency affects handling speed, not the necessary breadth of permissions.
  9. provisioning Turn 18. Provisioning is the actual assignment of the approved access; an approval message does not prove that assignment has already occurred.
  10. deprovisioning Turn 20. Deprovisioning removes access no longer required; assigning its ownership makes the temporary-access end condition actionable.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Jo: I need administrator access to finish the monthly reporting work. I can sign in, but the download gives me an access-denied message every time.
  2. Imani: Let us identify the required permission first. Which report are you downloading, and do you need to change anything in the system?
  3. Jo: It is the monthly sales summary. I only need to download it and analyze the file. I am not changing accounts or system settings.
  4. Imani: Then administrator rights would be broader than that business justification. There may be a reporting role that provides the specific action.
  5. Jo: I assumed signing in meant I already had access and the download error was just a technical fault. Are those separate parts of the process?
  6. Imani: Yes. Authentication gets you through the identity check. Authorization determines which resources and actions the account is allowed to use afterward.
  7. Jo: Could you assign the reporting role now and let me check whether it works? My manager needs the analysis, and I am only here temporarily.
  8. Imani: The system owner must approve it first. I also need to verify its actual entitlements, because the role name alone does not confirm that download is included.
  9. Jo: My manager can confirm the work is required. Does that approval cover the role too, or does the system owner need a separate request?
  10. Imani: In this process they have different responsibilities. The manager confirms the need, and the system owner is the approver for the system role.
  11. Jo: That is clear. The assignment ends on 30 November. I do not expect to need the system afterward unless the contract is formally extended.
  12. Imani: I will include an expiration aligned with that assignment and the required review route if it changes.
  13. Jo: While we wait, a colleague offered to download the file using their account. Is that the same as sharing their password so I can do it myself?
  14. Imani: Those are different proposals, and neither should bypass the approved process. Do not share credentials. Ask the report owner whether an authorized delivery route is permitted.
  15. Jo: I'll ask the report owner. In the meantime, can you flag the deadline and get the role review moving?
  16. Imani: Exactly. We can expedite review while preserving least privilege. I will submit the report name, required action, duration, and business justification together.
  17. Jo: Will the approval email mean I can download immediately, or is there another step after that?
  18. Imani: Approval and provisioning are separate states. After the role is assigned, confirm that the authorized download works with your own account.
  19. Jo: And at the end of November, the access should be removed unless an extension is reviewed. Please include who checks that so it is not forgotten.
  20. Imani: I will record deprovisioning ownership. For now the status is submitted for review, not granted, and we have a legitimate route that matches the work you actually need to do.
Dialogue in the book, page 28 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Requesting the reporting role

The lesson's temporary analyst needs a monthly download and has requested administrator access; a reporting role may suffice.

  1. Temporary analyst: Could you give me administrator access so I can download the monthly report?
  2. Access administrator: Please request the specific reporting function instead. We need to check the narrower role first.
  3. Temporary analyst: I can authenticate successfully, so why can't I already download the file?
  4. Access administrator: Authentication verifies your sign-in; authorization determines which actions your account may perform.
  5. Temporary analyst: Then could you assess whether the reporting role authorizes the download I need?
  6. Access administrator: Yes. Please include the report name, business purpose, and required access period in the request.
  7. Temporary analyst: I don't have approval yet. Should I describe the role as already agreed?
  8. Access administrator: No. The request should go to the relevant owner for approval under our access process.
  9. Temporary analyst: I'll submit the bounded request and explain why administrator rights may be unnecessary.
  10. Access administrator: I'll review the least-privilege option without promising access before authorization is confirmed.
Read access for a contractor review

A separate fictional project: a contractor must review one test dashboard for a week; editing is not part of the requested work.

  1. Project manager: Could we arrange dashboard access for the contractor's one-week review of our test results?
  2. IAM specialist: Which dashboard is needed, and does the contractor need to edit anything?
  3. Project manager: Only the test dashboard. The assignment is review, so editing isn't part of the request.
  4. IAM specialist: Then let's assess a read-only role rather than a broader workspace permission.
  5. Project manager: Can you copy another engineer's access to make the setup quicker?
  6. IAM specialist: That could include unrelated authorization. Least privilege means matching access to this specific work.
  7. Project manager: I'll identify the dashboard owner and ask them to review the read-only request.
  8. IAM specialist: Please include the one-week duration so removal or expiry can be addressed explicitly.
  9. Project manager: I'll also state that the start depends on approval, not assume today's access is guaranteed.
  10. IAM specialist: Good. I'll check the available role and return any missing information before provisioning.
Ending access after a temporary assignment

A separate fictional service team: an analyst's temporary reporting assignment has ended, but the corresponding role remains listed on the account.

  1. Service owner: The temporary assignment ended, but the reporting role is still listed on the analyst's account.
  2. Access administrator: Are you requesting removal of that role only, or closure of the entire account?
  3. Service owner: The role only. I don't have information about the analyst's other work.
  4. Access administrator: That's an important limit. Please identify the assignment and the role in your request.
  5. Service owner: Could you remove it before the next access review, once the owner approves?
  6. Access administrator: I'll check the authorization route and confirm timing rather than promise an unapproved change.
  7. Service owner: Would disabling authentication be a simpler way to enforce least privilege?
  8. Access administrator: It would affect all account use. Your request concerns one authorization, not every sign-in.
  9. Service owner: Understood. I'll keep the requested action narrow and ask the owner to confirm removal.
  10. Access administrator: I'll report the role's status after the approved action, including any work still pending.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A report request does not justify administrator access
  1. Check the answers. Read the required monthly-report action, assignment ending 30 November, and unverified fit of the reporting role.
  2. Switch roles. Distinguish the manager's confirmation of need from the system owner's approval; do not substitute administrator rights for the scoped request.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A contractor needs read-only dashboard access

A contractor needs to view one project dashboard for two weeks, not edit it. The project manager confirms the need; the dashboard owner approves access. No approval has yet been recorded.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Analyst: "The requested action is ___, not editing or administration."

Answer and explanation

C. viewing

The contractor's stated task is to view one dashboard; additional control is not justified by that task.

2. Manager: "I can confirm the business need, but the owner provides ___."

Answer and explanation

B. approval

The case separates confirmation of need from the owner's authorization decision.

3. Analyst: "The request will include a two-week ___."

Answer and explanation

A. duration

The access period should match the temporary task instead of becoming indefinite.

4. Manager: "Until review is complete, describe the access as ___."

Answer and explanation

C. pending

No approval has been recorded, so granted or active would overstate the current status.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.
