Lesson 03: Identity, Access, and Permissions
Original fictional case: A temporary analyst, Jo, needs to download a monthly report. Jo requests administrator access because the current account shows an access-denied message. A reporting role may allow the required download, but its exact permissions have not been verified. Imani handles access requests. The manager can confirm Jo's business need; the system owner approves the role. The assignment ends on 30 November.
Language workshop: A report request does not justify administrator access
Communication goal: Clarify the required task and refuse excessive access while offering a legitimate route.
Language guidance: Authentication establishes identity evidence; authorization governs permitted actions. Being able to sign in does not imply permission to download every report. A role name alone does not prove its actual rights.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Which report do you need, and what action must you perform? / You can sign in; the download permission is a different question. / I cannot justify administrator rights from the stated reporting task. / Let us verify whether the reporting role covers that download. / Your manager confirms the need; the system owner approves the role. / The requested access should match the assignment ending on 30 November. / The role may fit, but its permissions still need checking. / I will submit the task, resource, duration, and approval route together. / Please use your own approved account rather than another person's credentials. / Which entitlement is currently assigned to your account? / The request should provide the required action without unrelated control. / I can flag the deadline without bypassing the approval process. / After provisioning, confirm the authorized report action works. / Who will verify removal when the assignment finishes? / A reporting role and an administrator role serve different purposes. / Approval is pending; the request has been submitted, not granted.
Vocabulary: identity: The representation of a user or service in a system. / authentication: Establishing that a claimant controls the required identity evidence. / authorization: Permission to perform an action on a resource. / permission: An allowed action or access right. / role: A defined collection of access rights or responsibilities. / RBAC: Role-based access control; access organized through defined roles. / least privilege: Access limited to what is needed for the task. / administrator: A role with broad system-management permissions. / privileged access: Access allowing sensitive or powerful actions. / entitlement: A specific granted right to use a resource or capability. / provisioning: Creating or assigning accounts and access. / deprovisioning: Removing accounts or access no longer required. / access review: A check that granted access remains appropriate. / access request: A recorded request for specified permissions. / business justification: The work-related reason supporting a request. / approver: The person authorized to approve a request. / expiration: The point at which an entitlement or credential ends. / temporary access: Access granted for a defined limited period. / separation of duties: Dividing incompatible responsibilities to reduce risk. / SSO: Single sign-on; using one authentication session across services. / MFA: Multifactor authentication; use of distinct factor types. / service account: An identity used by an application or automated process. / audit log: A record of relevant actions for review. / access denied: A message indicating that an attempted action was not permitted.
Speaking task: Check the answers. Read the required monthly-report action, assignment ending 30 November, and unverified fit of the reporting role. Switch roles. Distinguish the manager's confirmation of need from the system owner's approval; do not substitute administrator rights for the scoped request. Complete the dashboard exchange. Retain viewing-only access for two weeks and pending approval.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

04Cloud, Infrastructure, and Cost-Aware OperationsThe cheaper estimate leaves out backup storage+

Your goal

Compare incomplete estimates and qualify a recommendation without inventing missing costs.

This lesson in the book, page 31 ↗

A Read the situation

The cheaper estimate leaves out backup storage

Two monthly cloud planning estimates cover the same application. Option A is $800 and excludes backup storage. Option B is $1,050 and includes backup storage. Transfer charges and support coverage have not been confirmed for either option. Jordan prepares the comparison; Sal owns infrastructure planning. These are fictional estimates, not provider prices. No purchasing decision has been approved.

Jordan: Cloud operations analyst / Sal: Infrastructure planning lead

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which cost difference is confirmed?

Answer and explanation

B. Only Option B includes backup storage.

The briefing identifies backup inclusion as different and leaves transfer and support unconfirmed.

2. What is the headline difference?

Answer and explanation

B. $250 per month

Subtracting $800 from $1,050 gives $250, but that is not a verified like-for-like saving.

3. Which conclusion is premature?

Answer and explanation

C. Option A is the cheaper complete solution.

The complete scope and missing charges are unresolved, so the cheaper headline cannot establish the cheaper complete service.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The $250 difference compares two headline estimates with different inclusions. It is not a verified saving for equivalent services. RPO describes a data-loss interval; RTO describes a restoration-time target.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
cloud service
Computing capability delivered through a service model.compare cloud services
infrastructure
The underlying computing and network resources.plan infrastructure capacity
instance
A running or configured unit of computing capacity.size a compute instance
virtual machine
A software-defined computing environment emulating a machine.provision a virtual machine
region
A provider-defined geographical deployment area.choose an approved region
availability zone
A provider-defined location grouping within a region.compare availability-zone designs
object storage
Storage that manages data as objects with metadata.estimate object-storage usage
block storage
Storage presented as addressable blocks.size block-storage volumes
snapshot
A point-in-time representation of data or system state.document a snapshot policy
backup
A retained copy intended to support recovery.include backup storage
retention period
The length of time data is kept.confirm the retention period
restore test
A check that retained data can be recovered as required.schedule a restore test
RPO
Recovery point objective; tolerated data-loss interval.agree on the RPO
RTO
Recovery time objective; target time to restore service.agree on the RTO
egress
Data leaving a defined network or service boundary.estimate egress charges
ingress
Data entering a defined network or service boundary.identify ingress traffic
consumption
Measured resource use over a period.forecast resource consumption
reservation
A commitment or capacity allocation under stated terms.compare reservation terms
on-demand
Resource use without the particular advance commitment being discussed.estimate on-demand usage
right-sizing
Matching resource allocation to workload needs.assess right-sizing options
cost allocation
Assignment of expenses to services or owners.document cost allocation
line item
An individual entry in an estimate or bill.compare each line item
exclusion
A cost or service left outside the stated scope.disclose estimate exclusions
total cost
Combined cost under explicitly defined inclusions.compare like-for-like total cost

A snapshot or stored backup does not prove that recovery has been tested. Provider pricing, region terminology, and commitment conditions vary; these fictional amounts teach comparison language, not purchasing advice.

C Notice the language

Comparable totals require comparable scope

A low headline figure can be accurate yet unsuitable for a decision when inclusions differ. Identify missing line items without accusing the estimator of deception. Ask for a like-for-like comparison and keep unknown amounts unknown.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Identify the mismatch
The estimates do not include the same services.
State the known difference
Option A excludes backup storage; Option B includes it.
Limit the arithmetic
The headline difference is $250, not a confirmed like-for-like saving.
Request a common basis
Can we compare both options against the same workload and retention assumptions?
Keep uncertainty visible
Transfer charges remain unconfirmed for both options.
Ask about coverage
Does support cover the hours and response level we need?
Separate cost and recovery
Including storage does not establish that restoration has been tested.
Bound the recommendation
I cannot recommend the lower total until the missing items are priced.
Check a unit
Is that per month, per stored unit, or per transfer volume?
Expose an assumption
Which usage pattern does this estimate assume?
Ask about commitments
What term or minimum spend is attached to that figure?
Preserve exclusions
Put the excluded items next to the headline total.
Avoid invented numbers
I will mark the amount unknown rather than enter zero.
Clarify an objective
Is that the tolerated data-loss interval or the restoration-time target?
Compare sensitivity
Show how the estimate changes under the agreed higher-use scenario.
Close the comparison
We will reconcile the scope before asking for a purchasing decision.
6 language and precision notes
Includes versus costs
A quoted amount and its included services must be reported together.
Unknown versus zero
"Not confirmed" is missing information, not a numerical zero.
Per
State the billing unit explicitly: per month, per gigabyte, or per request.
Would save
Conditional savings depend on the assumptions; name those assumptions before using would save.
Same as
"The same retention period as Option B" creates a precise comparison condition.
Not until
"Not until exclusions are priced" sets a clear prerequisite for the recommendation.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which comparison is defensible now?

Answer and explanation

A. A is $250 lower before its missing backup and other unconfirmed costs are resolved.

The correct statement gives the arithmetic while preserving the unequal scope and unknown items.

2. How should an unconfirmed transfer charge appear?

Answer and explanation

D. Unknown, with the required assumption identified

Missing information must remain explicit; entering zero or included would invent a cost assumption.

3. Which question concerns RPO?

Answer and explanation

C. How far back may the recovered data point be relative to the disruption?

Recovery point objective concerns the tolerated data-loss interval. Restoration duration is RTO; retention duration and storage volume answer other planning questions.

4. What does included backup storage fail to establish?

Answer and explanation

D. That a successful restore has been tested

Storing a backup and demonstrating recoverability are separate matters.

Check explanations in the book, page 91 ↗

E Conversations

The cheaper estimate leaves out backup storage

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Jordan: Cloud operations analyst / Sal: Infrastructure planning lead

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • audit trail
  • commitment
  • RPO
  • like-for-like
  • inclusions
  • consumption
  • headline difference
  • line item
  • unknown
  • retention period
  1. Jordan
    Option A is $800 a month and Option B is $1,050. I was going to recommend A as a $250 monthly saving for the application.
  2. Sal
    Before recommending it, compare the 1. I see backup storage in B, but not in A. Are they priced on the same basis?
  3. Jordan
    Not yet. A's estimate explicitly excludes backup storage. I also have not confirmed transfer charges or support coverage for either option, so the comparison is incomplete.
  4. Sal
    Then $250 is the 2, not an established saving. We should keep the arithmetic but qualify what it means.
  5. Jordan
    I will request the missing backup amount. Should I put zero against transfer charges until the provider confirms them, so the spreadsheet totals correctly?
  6. Sal
    No. Mark them 3 and record the assumed traffic. A working formula should not turn an unanswered pricing question into a free service.
  7. Jordan
    Understood. The workload estimate also assumes the current data volume. We expect growth, but the higher-use scenario has not been agreed with the application owner.
  8. Sal
    Obtain that assumption before adjusting the figure. Compare 4 on the same basis for both options rather than quietly giving one a smaller workload.
  9. Jordan
    For backups, is matching the storage amount enough? B includes a line for retained copies, but I have not seen evidence of a restore test.
  10. Sal
    Storage and recoverability are different. Ask about the agreed 5, recovery requirements, and the process for checking restoration under the approved plan.
  11. Jordan
    The application owner mentioned an RPO and an RTO. I want to make sure I ask the right question rather than using those labels interchangeably.
  12. Sal
    The 6 concerns how much recent data loss is tolerable. RTO concerns the target restoration time. Confirm both with the authorized owner.
  13. Jordan
    I will also check whether either price assumes a long commitment. A may be an on-demand estimate, while B may rely on an advance reservation.
  14. Sal
    Exactly. Any 7 belongs beside the monthly figure. A lower unit rate can involve obligations that change the decision.
  15. Jordan
    What about support? The summary says support available, but it does not specify coverage hours or which incidents receive the quoted response level.
  16. Sal
    Treat that as another 8 needing clarification. Availability is not the same as included coverage that meets this application's needs.
  17. Jordan
    I'll hold the recommendation. For today's note, I'll show the two prices and the missing items, with provisional above the totals.
  18. Sal
    Yes. We are aiming for 9 totals and clearly stated differences. We do not need to pretend the two options are identical if meaningful differences remain.
  19. Jordan
    I'll put the inclusions and unresolved amounts in one table. Can we review it again when the provider answers the backup and support questions?
  20. Sal
    Good. That creates an 10 for the recommendation. We can explain what changed when the final numbers arrive instead of defending a saving we never actually verified.

Answers and explanations
  1. inclusions Turn 2. Inclusions identify what the quoted amount covers; unequal included services make headline totals unsuitable as equivalent-service savings.
  2. headline difference Turn 4. The headline difference is the subtraction of the displayed estimates, not a verified saving after aligning their scope.
  3. unknown Turn 6. Unknown preserves missing information; zero would falsely assert that the charge has been established as nothing.
  4. consumption Turn 8. Consumption is actual or assumed resource use; estimates are meaningful only when the usage basis is explicit.
  5. retention period Turn 10. The retention period states how long copies are kept; it affects scope and cost but does not prove successful recovery.
  6. RPO Turn 12. Recovery point objective concerns the tolerated interval of data loss; it is distinct from the time needed to restore the service.
  7. commitment Turn 14. A commitment can impose term or spending conditions; the displayed monthly equivalent does not fully describe those obligations.
  8. line item Turn 16. A line item separates a cost or service component so its coverage and assumptions can be compared explicitly.
  9. like-for-like Turn 18. Like-for-like means comparing equivalent scope and assumptions, rather than selecting between superficially similar but different totals.
  10. audit trail Turn 20. An audit trail records the assumptions and decisions supporting the estimate, so later reviewers can understand why a recommendation was made.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Jordan: Option A is $800 a month and Option B is $1,050. I was going to recommend A as a $250 monthly saving for the application.
  2. Sal: Before recommending it, compare the inclusions. I see backup storage in B, but not in A. Are they priced on the same basis?
  3. Jordan: Not yet. A's estimate explicitly excludes backup storage. I also have not confirmed transfer charges or support coverage for either option, so the comparison is incomplete.
  4. Sal: Then $250 is the headline difference, not an established saving. We should keep the arithmetic but qualify what it means.
  5. Jordan: I will request the missing backup amount. Should I put zero against transfer charges until the provider confirms them, so the spreadsheet totals correctly?
  6. Sal: No. Mark them unknown and record the assumed traffic. A working formula should not turn an unanswered pricing question into a free service.
  7. Jordan: Understood. The workload estimate also assumes the current data volume. We expect growth, but the higher-use scenario has not been agreed with the application owner.
  8. Sal: Obtain that assumption before adjusting the figure. Compare consumption on the same basis for both options rather than quietly giving one a smaller workload.
  9. Jordan: For backups, is matching the storage amount enough? B includes a line for retained copies, but I have not seen evidence of a restore test.
  10. Sal: Storage and recoverability are different. Ask about the agreed retention period, recovery requirements, and the process for checking restoration under the approved plan.
  11. Jordan: The application owner mentioned an RPO and an RTO. I want to make sure I ask the right question rather than using those labels interchangeably.
  12. Sal: The RPO concerns how much recent data loss is tolerable. RTO concerns the target restoration time. Confirm both with the authorized owner.
  13. Jordan: I will also check whether either price assumes a long commitment. A may be an on-demand estimate, while B may rely on an advance reservation.
  14. Sal: Exactly. Any commitment belongs beside the monthly figure. A lower unit rate can involve obligations that change the decision.

  16. Sal: Treat that as another line item needing clarification. Availability is not the same as included coverage that meets this application's needs.

Dialogue in the book, page 37 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Comparing cloud estimates with the same backup scope

The lesson's cloud estimates are $800 and $1,050 monthly; only the second includes backup storage.

  1. Procurement analyst: The first cloud option is $800 monthly, so it looks $250 cheaper than the second.
  2. Cloud engineer: The second estimate includes backup storage. The first leaves that cost out.
  3. Procurement analyst: Then the price difference doesn't yet compare the same service scope?
  4. Cloud engineer: Correct. We need a comparable backup allowance before calling either option less expensive overall.
  5. Procurement analyst: Should I also check whether the VM size and region assumptions match?
  6. Cloud engineer: Yes. Those details affect the comparison, and we haven't established that they are identical.
  7. Procurement analyst: Could tagging tell us which team should own each cost after deployment?
  8. Cloud engineer: It could support cost allocation if we define and apply the tags consistently.
  9. Procurement analyst: I'll request an aligned estimate instead of recommending the apparently cheaper figure today.
  10. Cloud engineer: Then we can explain the real tradeoffs without hiding the missing backup component.
An idle test VM still appears on the estimate

A separate fictional test environment compares a continuously available VM with a proposed scheduled VM; restart time has not been measured.

  1. Product owner: The scheduled VM option looks attractive because we don't run tests overnight.
  2. Infrastructure engineer: It may reduce running time, but we need to compare availability and restart effort too.
  3. Product owner: Does stopping the VM mean every associated cloud cost stops as well?
  4. Infrastructure engineer: We shouldn't assume that. The estimate must identify storage and other retained resources separately.
  5. Product owner: The continuously available option avoids waiting for a restart, whereas the scheduled one may not.
  6. Infrastructure engineer: Exactly. We haven't measured that restart time or checked the test team's access pattern.
  7. Product owner: Let's ask about early test runs before accepting a narrower availability window.
  8. Infrastructure engineer: I'll also check resource tagging so the comparison includes the same environment in both cases.
  9. Product owner: Then we'll compare monthly cost, availability, and the unmeasured restart impact side by side.
  10. Infrastructure engineer: Good. We have a candidate saving, not a verified recommendation to switch.
Container packaging is not a complete operating cost

A separate fictional service review compares a container deployment proposal with an existing VM estimate; monitoring is included only in the VM estimate.

  1. Platform engineer: The container proposal lists compute costs, while the VM estimate also includes monitoring.
  2. Finance partner: Can we remove monitoring from the VM estimate to make the container proposal look competitive?
  3. Platform engineer: We should compare the required operating scope, not remove necessary items to favor one option.
  4. Finance partner: So both proposals need the same monitoring assumption before we discuss total cost?
  5. Platform engineer: Yes. Container packaging doesn't by itself establish a cheaper or simpler operating model.
  6. Finance partner: What other tradeoff should the decision group hear besides the monthly estimate?
  7. Platform engineer: We should assess the team's support responsibilities and deployment requirements for each option.
  8. Finance partner: Those haven't been costed yet, so I'll identify them as open items.
  9. Platform engineer: I'll ask the service owner to confirm the required scope and region assumptions.
  10. Finance partner: Then our comparison can separate priced components from operational questions that remain unresolved.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

The cheaper estimate leaves out backup storage
  1. Check the dialogue. Read $800 and $1,050 as unequal-scope estimates: $250 is their headline difference, not verified savings.
  2. Switch roles. Ask for missing backup, transfer and support terms; keep unknown charges distinct from zero.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

An annual software quote excludes migration

Quote A is $12,000 annually without migration support. Quote B is $14,000 with migration support. The required migration scope and any additional charges remain unconfirmed.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Analyst: "The $2,000 difference compares unequal ___."

Answer and explanation

B. scope

The two quotes differ in migration coverage, so their totals do not yet describe equivalent services.

2. Manager: "List migration as an ___ from A, not an assumed free service."

Answer and explanation

D. exclusion

Quote A explicitly leaves migration out; treating it as free would invent an inclusion.

3. Analyst: "I will keep the additional amount marked ___ until it is confirmed."

Answer and explanation

D. unknown

No amount has been established, so unknown is more accurate than zero.

4. Manager: "Then request a ___ comparison before recommending a purchase."

Answer and explanation

A. like-for-like

Aligning the migration scope and other assumptions makes the cost comparison decision-relevant.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 04: Cloud, Infrastructure, and Cost-Aware Operations
Original fictional case: Two monthly cloud planning estimates cover the same application. Option A is $800 and excludes backup storage. Option B is $1,050 and includes backup storage. Transfer charges and support coverage have not been confirmed for either option. Jordan prepares the comparison; Sal owns infrastructure planning. These are fictional estimates, not provider prices. No purchasing decision has been approved.
Language workshop: The cheaper estimate leaves out backup storage
Communication goal: Compare incomplete estimates and qualify a recommendation without inventing missing costs.
Language guidance: The $250 difference compares two headline estimates with different inclusions. It is not a verified saving for equivalent services. RPO describes a data-loss interval; RTO describes a restoration-time target.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The estimates do not include the same services. / Option A excludes backup storage; Option B includes it. / The headline difference is $250, not a confirmed like-for-like saving. / Can we compare both options against the same workload and retention assumptions? / Transfer charges remain unconfirmed for both options. / Does support cover the hours and response level we need? / Including storage does not establish that restoration has been tested. / I cannot recommend the lower total until the missing items are priced. / Is that per month, per stored unit, or per transfer volume? / Which usage pattern does this estimate assume? / What term or minimum spend is attached to that figure? / Put the excluded items next to the headline total. / I will mark the amount unknown rather than enter zero. / Is that the tolerated data-loss interval or the restoration-time target? / Show how the estimate changes under the agreed higher-use scenario. / We will reconcile the scope before asking for a purchasing decision.
Vocabulary: cloud service: Computing capability delivered through a service model. / infrastructure: The underlying computing and network resources. / instance: A running or configured unit of computing capacity. / virtual machine: A software-defined computing environment emulating a machine. / region: A provider-defined geographical deployment area. / availability zone: A provider-defined location grouping within a region. / object storage: Storage that manages data as objects with metadata. / block storage: Storage presented as addressable blocks. / snapshot: A point-in-time representation of data or system state. / backup: A retained copy intended to support recovery. / retention period: The length of time data is kept. / restore test: A check that retained data can be recovered as required. / RPO: Recovery point objective; tolerated data-loss interval. / RTO: Recovery time objective; target time to restore service. / egress: Data leaving a defined network or service boundary. / ingress: Data entering a defined network or service boundary. / consumption: Measured resource use over a period. / reservation: A commitment or capacity allocation under stated terms. / on-demand: Resource use without the particular advance commitment being discussed. / right-sizing: Matching resource allocation to workload needs. / cost allocation: Assignment of expenses to services or owners. / line item: An individual entry in an estimate or bill. / exclusion: A cost or service left outside the stated scope. / total cost: Combined cost under explicitly defined inclusions.
Speaking task: Check the dialogue. Read $800 and $1,050 as unequal-scope estimates: $250 is their headline difference, not verified savings. Switch roles. Ask for missing backup, transfer and support terms; keep unknown charges distinct from zero. Complete the annual-quote exchange. Identify the migration exclusion before requesting a like-for-like $12,000 versus $14,000 comparison.
Partner: Jordan: Cloud operations analyst / Sal: Infrastructure planning lead
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Quote A is $12,000 annually without migration support. Quote B is $14,000 with migration support. The required migration scope and any additional charges remain unconfirmed.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Jordan: I'll hold the recommendation. For today's note, I'll show the two prices and the missing items, with provisional above the totals.
Sal: Yes. We are aiming for like-for-like totals and clearly stated differences. We do not need to pretend the two options are identical if meaningful differences remain.
Jordan: I'll put the inclusions and unresolved amounts in one table. Can we review it again when the provider answers the backup and support questions?
Sal: Good. That creates an audit trail for the recommendation. We can explain what changed when the final numbers arrive instead of defending a saving we never actually verified.
END REFERENCE

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Communication goal: Compare incomplete estimates and qualify a recommendation without inventing missing costs.
Language guidance: The $250 difference compares two headline estimates with different inclusions. It is not a verified saving for equivalent services. RPO describes a data-loss interval; RTO describes a restoration-time target.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The estimates do not include the same services. / Option A excludes backup storage; Option B includes it. / The headline difference is $250, not a confirmed like-for-like saving. / Can we compare both options against the same workload and retention assumptions? / Transfer charges remain unconfirmed for both options. / Does support cover the hours and response level we need? / Including storage does not establish that restoration has been tested. / I cannot recommend the lower total until the missing items are priced. / Is that per month, per stored unit, or per transfer volume? / Which usage pattern does this estimate assume? / What term or minimum spend is attached to that figure? / Put the excluded items next to the headline total. / I will mark the amount unknown rather than enter zero. / Is that the tolerated data-loss interval or the restoration-time target? / Show how the estimate changes under the agreed higher-use scenario. / We will reconcile the scope before asking for a purchasing decision.
Vocabulary: cloud service: Computing capability delivered through a service model. / infrastructure: The underlying computing and network resources. / instance: A running or configured unit of computing capacity. / virtual machine: A software-defined computing environment emulating a machine. / region: A provider-defined geographical deployment area. / availability zone: A provider-defined location grouping within a region. / object storage: Storage that manages data as objects with metadata. / block storage: Storage presented as addressable blocks. / snapshot: A point-in-time representation of data or system state. / backup: A retained copy intended to support recovery. / retention period: The length of time data is kept. / restore test: A check that retained data can be recovered as required. / RPO: Recovery point objective; tolerated data-loss interval. / RTO: Recovery time objective; target time to restore service. / egress: Data leaving a defined network or service boundary. / ingress: Data entering a defined network or service boundary. / consumption: Measured resource use over a period. / reservation: A commitment or capacity allocation under stated terms. / on-demand: Resource use without the particular advance commitment being discussed. / right-sizing: Matching resource allocation to workload needs. / cost allocation: Assignment of expenses to services or owners. / line item: An individual entry in an estimate or bill. / exclusion: A cost or service left outside the stated scope. / total cost: Combined cost under explicitly defined inclusions.
Speaking task: Check the dialogue. Read $800 and $1,050 as unequal-scope estimates: $250 is their headline difference, not verified savings. Switch roles. Ask for missing backup, transfer and support terms; keep unknown charges distinct from zero. Complete the annual-quote exchange. Identify the migration exclusion before requesting a like-for-like $12,000 versus $14,000 comparison.
Partner: Jordan: Cloud operations analyst / Sal: Infrastructure planning lead
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Quote A is $12,000 annually without migration support. Quote B is $14,000 with migration support. The required migration scope and any additional charges remain unconfirmed.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Jordan: I'll hold the recommendation. For today's note, I'll show the two prices and the missing items, with provisional above the totals.
Sal: Yes. We are aiming for like-for-like totals and clearly stated differences. We do not need to pretend the two options are identical if meaningful differences remain.
Jordan: I'll put the inclusions and unresolved amounts in one table. Can we review it again when the provider answers the backup and support questions?
Sal: Good. That creates an audit trail for the recommendation. We can explain what changed when the final numbers arrive instead of defending a saving we never actually verified.
END REFERENCE
05Endpoints, Servers, Patching, and Configuration ManagementNinety percent installed is not complete+

Your goal

Report deployment states accurately and define evidence needed for completion.

This lesson in the book, page 40 ↗

A Read the situation

Ninety percent installed is not complete

A patch was assigned to 200 managed laptops. The console reports successful installation on 180, twelve laptops were offline, and eight returned installation errors. Post-installation verification has not been completed on all successful devices. Arun manages endpoints; Casey coordinates the rollout. A manager asks whether the deployment is complete. There is no approved exception for the remaining twenty laptops.

Arun: Endpoint administrator / Casey: Rollout coordinator

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. How many laptops report successful installation?

Answer and explanation

B. 180

The console reports 180 successes; offline devices and installation errors are separate remaining groups.

2. What is known about the remaining twenty?

Answer and explanation

B. Twelve were offline and eight returned errors.

The briefing gives two unresolved states and explicitly says no exception has been approved.

3. Why is complete premature?

Answer and explanation

C. Not all devices are installed and verified under the stated criteria.

Installation remains unresolved for twenty devices, and verification is not complete for all reported successes.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Assigned, downloaded, installed, restarted, and verified are not synonyms. A successful installation report may still require a restart or verification under the approved procedure. Report the actual evidence, not the most optimistic label.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
endpoint
A user device or other terminal system on a network.manage an endpoint
managed device
A device enrolled under an organization's management controls.verify the managed-device inventory
asset inventory
A record of devices and their relevant attributes.reconcile the asset inventory
patch
A software update intended to correct or change behavior.deploy an approved patch
patch level
The installed state of relevant software updates.confirm the patch level
deployment
Distribution and application of a configuration or update.track deployment progress
installation
Placement and setup of software on a system.confirm installation status
verification
Checking evidence that the required state has been achieved.complete post-installation verification
reboot
Restarting a system.identify a pending reboot
maintenance window
An approved period for planned service work.schedule a maintenance window
offline
Not currently connected or available to the management system.identify offline devices
retry
Another attempt after an earlier incomplete or failed action.schedule an authorized retry
error code
A code identifying a reported error condition.group installation error codes
compliance state
A device's status against defined requirements.report the compliance state
configuration baseline
The agreed reference configuration.compare with the configuration baseline
configuration drift
Difference from the expected configuration over time.detect configuration drift
exception
An approved departure from a requirement under stated conditions.document an approved exception
remediation
Work to correct a deficiency or failure.assign remediation ownership
deployment ring
A defined group receiving a staged rollout.review a deployment ring
pilot group
A limited group used to test a change before expansion.validate the pilot group
supersedence
Replacement of an earlier update by another.check patch supersedence
dependency
A prerequisite needed for another action to succeed.verify installation dependencies
rollback
Return to an earlier configuration or version.confirm rollback readiness
completion criterion
A condition defining when work counts as complete.state the completion criterion

An offline device is not automatically an installation failure or an approved exception. An exception requires an authorized decision. Keep the inventory denominator stable unless an approved scope change is recorded.

C Notice the language

Use state labels that preserve remaining work

A broad word such as done can hide devices that were offline, failed, or still require verification. Report mutually understandable states and counts. Do not describe a percentage as completion unless the agreed criteria actually permit it.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Report the counts
Installation succeeded on 180 of 200 managed laptops.
Separate remaining states
Twelve were offline; eight returned installation errors.
Qualify completion
Verification is not yet complete on all reported successes.
Reject an overstatement
I would report progress, not mark the deployment complete.
Preserve the denominator
The planned scope remains 200 devices unless a change is approved.
Distinguish an exception
Offline status is not an approved exception.
Ask for error evidence
Which error codes are shared across the eight failures?
Set the next action
Assign owners for the offline follow-up, errors, and verification.
Check a restart
Does the required state depend on a pending reboot?
Avoid a false category
Keep installation failure separate from devices we could not reach.
Name the criterion
What evidence must every in-scope device provide for completion?
Limit the percentage
Ninety percent installed does not mean the full rollout is verified.
Clarify a retry
A retry is scheduled; its outcome is not yet known.
Record a change
Any removal from scope needs a documented reason and approval.
Track recovery
Keep the approved rollback route available during the rollout.
Close honestly
The next update will show verified devices and each unresolved group.
6 language and precision notes
Of versus remaining
"180 of 200" reports success against scope; "20 remaining" identifies unresolved work.
Reported versus verified
"Reports successful installation" describes a console state; "verified" names a completed check.
Yet
"Not yet complete" preserves unfinished work without predicting when it will finish.
Separate nouns
Use offline devices and failed installations as separate categories when the evidence distinguishes them.
Unless
"The scope remains 200 unless changed by approval" states the condition for revising the denominator.
Scheduled versus completed
A scheduled retry is a plan, not evidence of a successful installation.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which status statement is accurate?

Answer and explanation

A. 180 installed; twelve offline; eight errors; verification still in progress.

The correct statement preserves each known state without inventing completion, exceptions, or verification failures.

2. What percentage reports successful installation?

Answer and explanation

D. 90 percent

Dividing 180 successful reports by 200 in-scope devices gives 90 percent.

3. Which action would distort the report?

Answer and explanation

C. Removing offline devices from scope without an approved change

Unapproved removal changes the denominator to improve appearance rather than reflect the agreed scope.

4. Which statement describes a plan rather than a result?

Answer and explanation

B. A retry is scheduled for the eight errors.

Scheduled identifies intended future work; it does not establish the retry outcome.

Check explanations in the book, page 93 ↗

E Conversations

Ninety percent installed is not complete

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Arun: Endpoint administrator / Casey: Rollout coordinator

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • exception
  • inventory
  • offline
  • error codes
  • reboot
  • denominator
  • completion criterion
  • criteria
  • installation
  • remediation
  1. Casey
    The manager wants to know whether the laptop deployment is complete. The console shows 180 successes out of 200. Can I mark the rollout done?
  2. Arun
    Not yet. We need to distinguish 1 from verification. Twelve devices were offline and eight returned errors, so twenty still lack successful installation reports.
  3. Casey
    I could say ninety percent complete and list the remaining devices underneath. Would that be clearer than calling the whole deployment finished for the manager?
  4. Arun
    Say ninety percent report installed. Our 2 also includes verification, and that check has not finished on every reported success.
  5. Casey
    What should we call the twelve offline laptops? They may simply belong to people who were away. I do not want to count them as failed installations.
  6. Arun
    Keep them in an 3 group. They remain in scope, but their status differs from the eight devices that actually returned installation errors.
  7. Casey
    Someone suggested excluding the offline devices from the total for now. That would make the progress figure look closer to the target while we contact their owners.
  8. Arun
    The 4 remains 200 unless an approved scope change says otherwise. Being inconvenient to reach is not a reason to remove a device from the report.
  9. Casey
    Understood. For the eight errors, do we have enough information to promise a successful retry today, or do we need a separate technical review first?
  10. Arun
    Review the 5 first. A shared count can hide different causes, and a scheduled retry is not evidence that the installation will complete.
  11. Casey
    I will ask for owners and next actions for both groups. What about the successful devices that still need a restart before their new state takes effect?
  12. Arun
    Record any pending 6 separately under the approved process. We should not describe a device as verified while a required state transition remains unfinished.
  13. Casey
    If a laptop cannot be updated during this window because of a documented application dependency, can the team mark it as an exception in the report?
  14. Arun
    Only after the authorized 7 decision. Record the reason, owner, and review condition; do not convert an unresolved error into an approved departure automatically.
  15. Casey
    I'll show those states separately. But can a device be both installed and pending verification? I don't want the totals to exceed two hundred.
  16. Arun
    Yes. Pending verification can be a subset of the installed group, not extra devices to add to it. Reconcile the 8 and state which counts overlap.
  17. Casey
    Can you send the verified count before this afternoon's review, with an owner for each outstanding group? That's what the manager will ask for.
  18. Arun
    Yes. I will include the current evidence and 9 status. Where an action depends on a user or another team, I will make that dependency visible.
  19. Casey
    Then my message will say installation is progressing, twenty devices lack success reports, verification remains open, and no exception has been approved for those twenty.
  20. Arun
    That is accurate. We will close the rollout only when the agreed 10 are met or an authorized scope decision changes them. Clear state labels let the manager decide without being misled by done.

Answers and explanations
  1. installation Turn 2. Installation is the software's reported setup state; it is not automatically the complete verified deployment state.
  2. completion criterion Turn 4. The completion criterion defines the evidence required for done; an installation percentage does not replace the required verification.
  3. offline Turn 6. Offline identifies devices unavailable to the management system; it does not assert that an installation was attempted and failed.
  4. denominator Turn 8. The denominator is the total in-scope count; changing it without approval would make the percentage misleading.
  5. error codes Turn 10. Error codes identify reported conditions that may require different responses; the count alone cannot establish that a retry will succeed.
  6. reboot Turn 12. A reboot is a restart; if required for the approved state, installation alone is not enough to claim verification.
  7. exception Turn 14. An exception is an approved departure with conditions, not a label the team applies simply because a device remains unresolved.
  8. inventory Turn 16. The inventory identifies the in-scope devices and their attributes; a verification-pending subset must not be added to the installed group as if it contained additional devices.
  9. remediation Turn 18. Remediation is the work assigned to correct outstanding deficiencies; naming it does not claim that those deficiencies are already corrected.
  10. criteria Turn 20. Criteria are the conditions defining acceptable completion; clear reporting follows those conditions rather than an attractive percentage.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Casey: The manager wants to know whether the laptop deployment is complete. The console shows 180 successes out of 200. Can I mark the rollout done?
  2. Arun: Not yet. We need to distinguish installation from verification. Twelve devices were offline and eight returned errors, so twenty still lack successful installation reports.
  3. Casey: I could say ninety percent complete and list the remaining devices underneath. Would that be clearer than calling the whole deployment finished for the manager?
  4. Arun: Say ninety percent report installed. Our completion criterion also includes verification, and that check has not finished on every reported success.

  6. Arun: Keep them in an offline group. They remain in scope, but their status differs from the eight devices that actually returned installation errors.
  7. Casey: Someone suggested excluding the offline devices from the total for now. That would make the progress figure look closer to the target while we contact their owners.
  8. Arun: The denominator remains 200 unless an approved scope change says otherwise. Being inconvenient to reach is not a reason to remove a device from the report.
  9. Casey: Understood. For the eight errors, do we have enough information to promise a successful retry today, or do we need a separate technical review first?
  10. Arun: Review the error codes first. A shared count can hide different causes, and a scheduled retry is not evidence that the installation will complete.
  11. Casey: I will ask for owners and next actions for both groups. What about the successful devices that still need a restart before their new state takes effect?

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

One hundred eighty is not the whole fleet

The lesson's patch deployment reached 180 of 200 laptops; twelve were offline and eight reported installation errors.

  1. IT manager: Can I tell the department that the laptop patch deployment is complete?
  2. Endpoint administrator: Not yet. It reached 180 of 200 managed laptops; twenty still need attention.
  3. IT manager: Are all twenty failures, or do we need different follow-up actions?
  4. Endpoint administrator: Twelve were offline, while eight reported installation errors. Those are different pending categories.
  5. IT manager: Does the MDM report confirm that every successful installation has been functionally tested?
  6. Endpoint administrator: It reports deployment status. We shouldn't extend that into an unverified claim about every function.
  7. IT manager: Please separate the asset inventory count from the installation and verification status.
  8. Endpoint administrator: I will. I'll review the error group and arrange follow-up for the offline laptops.
  9. IT manager: Can you promise all twenty will be finished in the next maintenance window?
  10. Endpoint administrator: I can plan follow-up, but availability and error resolution must be checked before committing.
A server patch installed but restart pending

A separate fictional server maintenance record shows the update installed; the planned restart and application check remain pending.

  1. Service owner: The patch shows installed. May I announce that maintenance is finished for the application?
  2. Server administrator: The installation is complete, but the restart and application check are still pending.
  3. Service owner: Then installed and ready for normal service are different states in this record?
  4. Server administrator: Yes. We need to communicate the remaining steps within the agreed maintenance window.
  5. Service owner: Has anyone confirmed that the application will pass its check after the restart?
  6. Server administrator: No. That check hasn't happened, so a successful result cannot be reported yet.
  7. Service owner: The update is installed; the restart and application check are still pending.
  8. Server administrator: Please don't imply a new outage or a failed check either; neither is recorded.
  9. Service owner: Understood. Who will supply the next status once the planned steps are complete?
  10. Server administrator: I'll provide the restart result and the application owner's check, with any unresolved issue separated.
A device missing from the managed inventory

A separate fictional inventory review finds a listed laptop absent from the MDM report; its current location and management status are unknown.

  1. Asset coordinator: This laptop appears in the asset inventory but not in the current MDM report.
  2. Endpoint administrator: Then we can't include it among devices with confirmed patch status from that report.
  3. Asset coordinator: Could we mark it retired so the deployment percentage looks complete?
  4. Endpoint administrator: No. Its status is unknown. A missing management record doesn't establish retirement.
  5. Asset coordinator: What work is complete in this review, and what remains for follow-up?
  6. Endpoint administrator: We've identified the discrepancy. We still need to confirm ownership, location, and management status.
  7. Asset coordinator: I'll ask the recorded owner about the device through our normal asset process.
  8. Endpoint administrator: I'll check whether the management record uses another identifier without changing the inventory prematurely.
  9. Asset coordinator: Our update should show one unresolved inventory item, not an assumed patch success.
  10. Endpoint administrator: Exactly. We'll reconcile the records before revising the managed-device count or completion claim.

  2. Switch roles. Treat pending verification as a possible subset of installed devices, not extra inventory; retain the approved 200-device scope.

A server update is installed but not verified

An approved server update reports successful installation. A required restart is pending, and the service check has not run. The maintenance owner must receive an accurate status.

Answer and explanation

A. reboot

The required restart has not occurred, so the installation status cannot imply the final operational state.

2. Owner: "Then the service check is still part of ___."

Answer and explanation

A. verification

Verification checks that the required state and service behavior have actually been achieved.

3. Engineer: "I will describe the update as in progress, not ___."

Answer and explanation

B. complete

The required restart and service check remain unfinished, so complete would overstate the evidence.

4. Owner: "Keep the approved recovery route available until the ___ are met."

Answer and explanation

C. criteria

The completion conditions, not the installation message alone, determine when the work can be closed.

Lesson 05: Endpoints, Servers, Patching, and Configuration Management
Original fictional case: A patch was assigned to 200 managed laptops. The console reports successful installation on 180, twelve laptops were offline, and eight returned installation errors. Post-installation verification has not been completed on all successful devices. Arun manages endpoints; Casey coordinates the rollout. A manager asks whether the deployment is complete. There is no approved exception for the remaining twenty laptops.
Language workshop: Ninety percent installed is not complete
Communication goal: Report deployment states accurately and define evidence needed for completion.
Language guidance: Assigned, downloaded, installed, restarted, and verified are not synonyms. A successful installation report may still require a restart or verification under the approved procedure. Report the actual evidence, not the most optimistic label.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Installation succeeded on 180 of 200 managed laptops. / Twelve were offline; eight returned installation errors. / Verification is not yet complete on all reported successes. / I would report progress, not mark the deployment complete. / The planned scope remains 200 devices unless a change is approved. / Offline status is not an approved exception. / Which error codes are shared across the eight failures? / Assign owners for the offline follow-up, errors, and verification. / Does the required state depend on a pending reboot? / Keep installation failure separate from devices we could not reach. / What evidence must every in-scope device provide for completion? / Ninety percent installed does not mean the full rollout is verified. / A retry is scheduled; its outcome is not yet known. / Any removal from scope needs a documented reason and approval. / Keep the approved rollback route available during the rollout. / The next update will show verified devices and each unresolved group.
Vocabulary: endpoint: A user device or other terminal system on a network. / managed device: A device enrolled under an organization's management controls. / asset inventory: A record of devices and their relevant attributes. / patch: A software update intended to correct or change behavior. / patch level: The installed state of relevant software updates. / deployment: Distribution and application of a configuration or update. / installation: Placement and setup of software on a system. / verification: Checking evidence that the required state has been achieved. / reboot: Restarting a system. / maintenance window: An approved period for planned service work. / offline: Not currently connected or available to the management system. / retry: Another attempt after an earlier incomplete or failed action. / error code: A code identifying a reported error condition. / compliance state: A device's status against defined requirements. / configuration baseline: The agreed reference configuration. / configuration drift: Difference from the expected configuration over time. / exception: An approved departure from a requirement under stated conditions. / remediation: Work to correct a deficiency or failure. / deployment ring: A defined group receiving a staged rollout. / pilot group: A limited group used to test a change before expansion. / supersedence: Replacement of an earlier update by another. / dependency: A prerequisite needed for another action to succeed. / rollback: Return to an earlier configuration or version. / completion criterion: A condition defining when work counts as complete.
Speaking task: Check the answers. Read 180 installed, twelve offline and eight errors out of 200; distinguish the 90-percent installation rate from completion. Switch roles. Treat pending verification as a possible subset of installed devices, not extra inventory; retain the approved 200-device scope. Complete the server exchange. Keep the required restart and service check pending instead of reporting the update complete.
Partner: Arun: Endpoint administrator / Casey: Rollout coordinator
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: An approved server update reports successful installation. A required restart is pending, and the service check has not run. The maintenance owner must receive an accurate status.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Casey: Can you send the verified count before this afternoon's review, with an owner for each outstanding group? That's what the manager will ask for.
Arun: Yes. I will include the current evidence and remediation status. Where an action depends on a user or another team, I will make that dependency visible.
Casey: Then my message will say installation is progressing, twenty devices lack success reports, verification remains open, and no exception has been approved for those twenty.
Arun: That is accurate. We will close the rollout only when the agreed criteria are met or an authorized scope decision changes them. Clear state labels let the manager decide without being misled by done.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

06Security Operations, Risk, and Incident ResponseAn unusual sign-in is not yet a confirmed compromise+

Your goal

Escalate a security observation with urgency, accurate scope, and controlled evidence.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

An unusual sign-in is not yet a confirmed compromise

An alert flags an unusual sign-in for a staff account at 09:12 UTC. The account owner has not confirmed whether the activity was legitimate. No data-access review has occurred. Bea detected the alert; Tomas coordinates the authorized response team. The team has a restricted evidence channel and an on-call contact. Only authorized responders decide containment actions in this fictional organization.

Bea: Security monitoring analyst / Tomas: Incident response coordinator

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is confirmed?

Answer and explanation

A. An unusual-sign-in alert occurred at 09:12 UTC.

The alert and its timestamp are known; legitimacy, compromise, and data impact remain unconfirmed.

2. Who decides containment under this case's process?

Answer and explanation

B. Authorized responders

The briefing assigns containment decisions to authorized responders, not to every person seeing the alert.

3. Where should detailed evidence be placed?

Answer and explanation

C. The approved restricted evidence channel

Use the designated evidence route. An unconfirmed classification, a personal copy or partial redaction does not make another destination approved.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

An alert is a signal to assess, not automatic proof of compromise. A location estimate is not proof of a person's physical location. Preserve the difference between observed evidence, an interpretation, and an authorized classification.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
alert
A signal that a condition may require attention.triage an alert
event
An observed occurrence in a system.correlate security events
indicator
A piece of information that may point to relevant activity.assess an indicator
compromise
Unauthorized access or loss of a system's trusted state.investigate suspected compromise
breach
A security or privacy violation whose exact meaning depends on context.avoid an unverified breach claim
false positive
An alert or finding that identifies a condition not actually present.investigate a possible false positive
sign-in log
A record of authentication attempts or sessions.preserve the sign-in log
session
A period or context of authenticated interaction.review the account session
geolocation
An estimate of geographical position from available signals.qualify a geolocation estimate
anomaly
A departure from an expected pattern.assess a sign-in anomaly
SOC
Security operations center; a function coordinating monitoring and response.notify the SOC
on-call
Assigned availability to respond during a defined period.contact the on-call responder
escalation threshold
A condition requiring referral or higher attention.apply the escalation threshold
containment
Actions intended to limit an incident's consequences.assign containment authority
eradication
Removal of identified causes or malicious elements under a response process.distinguish eradication from containment
recovery
Restoration of services or operations after disruption.coordinate recovery communication
evidence preservation
Keeping relevant records intact for authorized review.follow evidence-preservation requirements
chain of custody
A record of evidence handling and transfers.maintain chain of custody
timeline
An ordered account of events with times.build an incident timeline
scope assessment
Determination of affected assets, accounts, or data.conduct a scope assessment
severity
The assessed seriousness of an incident or finding.review incident severity
notification
Communication to designated recipients under defined rules.confirm notification ownership
need to know
A principle restricting information to necessary recipients.share on a need-to-know basis
post-incident review
Examination of an incident and lessons after response.schedule a post-incident review

Containment limits consequences; eradication addresses identified causes or malicious elements; recovery restores operations. These labels describe distinct concerns, not a universal sequence that replaces local response procedures.

C Notice the language

Urgency and uncertainty can coexist

You can escalate promptly without labeling an unverified event a breach. State the alert, known facts, unanswered questions, and available evidence. An appropriately qualified report helps responders act; qualification is not a reason to delay notification.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Lead with the event
An unusual-sign-in alert was recorded at 09:12 UTC.
State the unknown
The account owner has not yet confirmed the activity.
Escalate promptly
I am referring this through the approved response channel now.
Avoid a premature label
We have not established compromise or data exposure.
Protect the record
The complete evidence is in the restricted channel.
Name the authority
The authorized responder will decide containment actions.
Preserve timing
Keep the original timestamp and its time zone in the timeline.
Set the next update
The next update will distinguish confirmed findings from open questions.
Qualify location
The alert reports a location estimate, not verified physical travel.
Check scope
Which accounts and assets have actually been reviewed?
Limit distribution
Share account details only with the designated recipients.
Avoid reassurance without evidence
An unconfirmed alert is not the same as a cleared alert.
Record a handoff
Please confirm who has accepted response ownership.
Separate decisions
Technical response and external notification have different owners.
Preserve evidence
Follow the approved handling process before altering relevant records.
Close accurately
The finding remains under review; no final classification has been made.
6 language and precision notes
Suspected versus confirmed
These modifiers mark different evidence states and must survive shortened updates.
Has not yet
This describes an unfinished confirmation, not proof that the activity was unauthorized.
Who owns
Ask for a named responsibility rather than assuming that everyone on a message is acting.
According to
"According to the alert" attributes a reported signal without treating it as verified reality.
Scope of no
"No review has occurred" describes missing investigation, not absence of data access.
Urgent but qualified
A direct escalation can include "unconfirmed" without weakening the instruction to review promptly.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which initial report is accurate?

Answer and explanation

D. Unusual-sign-in alert at 09:12 UTC; owner confirmation and scope review pending.

The accurate report supplies the known event and missing checks without prematurely confirming or dismissing the issue.

2. What does "owner confirmation pending" establish?

Answer and explanation

B. Confirmation has not been completed.

Pending identifies an unfinished check and does not supply either possible outcome.

3. Which question clarifies response ownership?

Answer and explanation

D. Who has accepted responsibility for the containment decision under the approved process?

The question asks for an accepted, authorized decision responsibility. Seeing an alert, sending an update or possessing access does not establish that ownership.

4. Which statement overclaims scope?

Answer and explanation

C. Every account is affected because one alert fired.

A single account alert cannot establish that every account is affected without further investigation.

Answers and explanations
  1. restricted channel Turn 2. The restricted channel keeps detailed account evidence available to authorized responders without broadly distributing it.
  2. alert Turn 4. An alert is a signal requiring assessment; it does not alone establish unauthorized access or compromise.
  3. geolocation Turn 6. Geolocation is an estimate based on available signals; it is not verified evidence of the person's physical location.
  4. scope assessment Turn 8. A scope assessment determines what accounts, assets, or data are affected; an unreviewed area cannot be declared unaffected.
  5. containment Turn 10. Containment limits possible consequences and is an operational decision for the role authorized in this case.
  6. timeline Turn 12. A timeline orders events using comparable timestamps; inconsistent time zones can lead reviewers to associate the wrong records.
  7. classification Turn 14. Classification records the assessed type or status of the event; one new statement should be handled through the approved review rather than automatic closure.
  8. notification Turn 16. Notification decisions concern designated recipients and applicable obligations; they have authorized owners separate from the initial alert reporter.
  9. need-to-know Turn 18. Need-to-know limits detailed information to people who require it for their role, while allowing a suitable broader status summary.
  10. under review Turn 20. Under review accurately marks an unresolved assessment; it neither clears the alert nor confirms a breach before the evidence supports either result.
