Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Handing over an unconfirmed sign-in alert

The lesson's analyst hands an unusual sign-in alert to the response team; the account owner has not confirmed the activity.

  1. Security analyst: I'm handing over an unusual sign-in alert. The account owner hasn't confirmed whether the activity was theirs.
  2. Incident responder: Is compromise confirmed, or are we reviewing an alert that still needs validation?
  3. Security analyst: Only the alert is confirmed. I haven't established unauthorized access from it.
  4. Incident responder: Please identify the alert reference and where the relevant audit log can be reviewed.
  5. Security analyst: I'll attach those references in the restricted incident record, not copy sensitive details into general chat.
  6. Incident responder: What action are you asking the response team to take next?
  7. Security analyst: Review the sign-in evidence and coordinate the owner check through the response process.
  8. Incident responder: I'll take that review. Has any containment action already been reported in this handoff?
  9. Security analyst: No containment is stated here. Please verify the record before assuming any action occurred.
  10. Incident responder: Understood: unconfirmed activity, evidence references in the incident record, and validation assigned to me.
A vulnerability result without an exploit finding

A separate fictional security review: a scanner flags one test server; no exploitation evidence has been examined.

  1. Vulnerability analyst: The scanner flagged one test server. I'm handing the finding to you for validation.
  2. Server owner: Does the finding mean someone has already exploited the server's vulnerability?
  3. Vulnerability analyst: No. The scan result is not evidence that exploitation occurred, and that question hasn't been examined.
  4. Server owner: What information will help me confirm whether the finding applies to this asset?
  5. Vulnerability analyst: The scanner reference, asset identifier, and reported software detail are in the restricted review record.
  6. Server owner: I'll verify the asset configuration. Should I also promise a patch date now?
  7. Vulnerability analyst: Please assess the finding and maintenance requirements first. We haven't established a feasible date.
  8. Server owner: Then my immediate task is validation, with remediation planning dependent on the result.
  9. Vulnerability analyst: Correct. Return the evidence and any unresolved questions so the risk owner can review them.
  10. Server owner: I accept that handoff and won't describe the scan as a confirmed compromise.
A SIEM alert with an incomplete time range

A separate fictional monitoring handoff: an analyst has saved an alert and ten minutes of audit logs; earlier activity has not been reviewed.

  1. Monitoring analyst: I've saved the SIEM alert and ten minutes of related audit logs for your review.
  2. Response lead: Does that cover the full event, or only the window you examined so far?
  3. Monitoring analyst: Only that window. I haven't reviewed earlier activity, so the beginning is unknown.
  4. Response lead: Thank you. We shouldn't call the available extract a complete incident timeline.
  5. Monitoring analyst: I'll mark the coverage limits and link the original alert in the restricted record.
  6. Response lead: What remains for the next analyst besides checking the entries already collected?
  7. Monitoring analyst: They need to assess whether a wider time range is relevant and gather it appropriately.
  8. Response lead: I'll assign that assessment and preserve the existing extract as part of the evidence.
  9. Monitoring analyst: Could you repeat the open point so I know the handoff is clear?
  10. Response lead: The alert and ten-minute extract are available; event scope and earlier activity remain unestablished.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

An unusual sign-in is not yet a confirmed compromise
  1. Check the dialogue. Read the 09:12 UTC alert without claiming confirmed compromise or a cleared false positive.
  2. Switch roles. Preserve pending account-owner confirmation and data-access review; use the restricted evidence route.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A suspicious email is reported but not analyzed

An employee reports a suspicious email. The attachment has not been analyzed, and no interaction has been confirmed. The approved reporting route accepts the original message for authorized review.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Analyst: "The email is reported suspicious; malicious content is not yet ___."

Answer and explanation

C. confirmed

A report identifies a concern, but analysis has not established what the attachment contains.

2. Coordinator: "Use the approved reporting ___ for the original message."

Answer and explanation

C. route

The defined route sends the evidence to authorized reviewers instead of encouraging informal redistribution.

3. Analyst: "Whether anyone interacted with it remains ___."

Answer and explanation

C. unknown

No interaction has been confirmed, which is not evidence that interaction either did or did not occur.

4. Coordinator: "Keep the wider update limited to the audience's need to ___."

Answer and explanation

B. know

The need-to-know principle restricts unnecessary distribution of potentially sensitive incident information.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.

4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Lesson 06: Security Operations, Risk, and Incident Response
Original fictional case: An alert flags an unusual sign-in for a staff account at 09:12 UTC. The account owner has not confirmed whether the activity was legitimate. No data-access review has occurred. Bea detected the alert; Tomas coordinates the authorized response team. The team has a restricted evidence channel and an on-call contact. Only authorized responders decide containment actions in this fictional organization.
Language workshop: An unusual sign-in is not yet a confirmed compromise
Communication goal: Escalate a security observation with urgency, accurate scope, and controlled evidence.
Language guidance: An alert is a signal to assess, not automatic proof of compromise. A location estimate is not proof of a person's physical location. Preserve the difference between observed evidence, an interpretation, and an authorized classification.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): An unusual-sign-in alert was recorded at 09:12 UTC. / The account owner has not yet confirmed the activity. / I am referring this through the approved response channel now. / We have not established compromise or data exposure. / The complete evidence is in the restricted channel. / The authorized responder will decide containment actions. / Keep the original timestamp and its time zone in the timeline. / The next update will distinguish confirmed findings from open questions. / The alert reports a location estimate, not verified physical travel. / Which accounts and assets have actually been reviewed? / Share account details only with the designated recipients. / An unconfirmed alert is not the same as a cleared alert. / Please confirm who has accepted response ownership. / Technical response and external notification have different owners. / Follow the approved handling process before altering relevant records. / The finding remains under review; no final classification has been made.
Vocabulary: alert: A signal that a condition may require attention. / event: An observed occurrence in a system. / indicator: A piece of information that may point to relevant activity. / compromise: Unauthorized access or loss of a system's trusted state. / breach: A security or privacy violation whose exact meaning depends on context. / false positive: An alert or finding that identifies a condition not actually present. / sign-in log: A record of authentication attempts or sessions. / session: A period or context of authenticated interaction. / geolocation: An estimate of geographical position from available signals. / anomaly: A departure from an expected pattern. / SOC: Security operations center; a function coordinating monitoring and response. / on-call: Assigned availability to respond during a defined period. / escalation threshold: A condition requiring referral or higher attention. / containment: Actions intended to limit an incident's consequences. / eradication: Removal of identified causes or malicious elements under a response process. / recovery: Restoration of services or operations after disruption. / evidence preservation: Keeping relevant records intact for authorized review. / chain of custody: A record of evidence handling and transfers. / timeline: An ordered account of events with times. / scope assessment: Determination of affected assets, accounts, or data. / severity: The assessed seriousness of an incident or finding. / notification: Communication to designated recipients under defined rules. / need to know: A principle restricting information to necessary recipients. / post-incident review: Examination of an incident and lessons after response.
Speaking task: Check the dialogue. Read the 09:12 UTC alert without claiming confirmed compromise or a cleared false positive. Switch roles. Preserve pending account-owner confirmation and data-access review; use the restricted evidence route. Complete the email exchange. Keep attachment content and user interaction unconfirmed.
Partner: Bea: Security monitoring analyst / Tomas: Incident response coordinator
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: An employee reports a suspicious email. The attachment has not been analyzed, and no interaction has been confirmed. The approved reporting route accepts the original message for authorized review.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Bea: I'll send the business lead a short status update: who has the case, which checks are running, and what we still don't know. No account details.
Tomas: Exactly. Use a need-to-know summary for the audience. Clear communication does not mean every recipient receives every piece of evidence.
Bea: I'll submit it now. If the on-call responder hasn't accepted it, I'll follow the escalation route rather than leave it in the queue.
Tomas: That is the right distinction. Keep the finding under review until the authorized decision is recorded, and preserve the evidence and uncertainty in every shortened update.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

07Change, Release, Problem, and Post-Incident CommunicationCorrect the maintenance date before users act on it+

Your goal

Replace an inaccurate notice, acknowledge impact, and confirm the controlling record.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

Correct the maintenance date before users act on it

A maintenance notice sent to users says Tuesday from 8 to 9 p.m. The approved change record says Thursday from 8 to 9 p.m., local branch time. The work has not begun. One team moved its export schedule in response to the incorrect notice. Mina sent the notice; Owen owns change coordination. A correction must reach the original recipients and clearly replace the earlier message.

Mina: Service communications analyst / Owen: Change coordinator

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which window is approved?

Answer and explanation

B. Thursday, 8 to 9 p.m., local branch time

The approved change record specifies Thursday evening in local branch time.

2. What actually changed?

Answer and explanation

D. The notice contained an error; the approved window did not change.

The case distinguishes a mistaken communication from a change to the authorized schedule.

3. Which consequence is known?

Answer and explanation

D. One team moved its export schedule.

The briefing identifies one concrete downstream scheduling consequence without establishing wider damage.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A mistaken notice is not automatically a changed authorization. "The date has moved" would imply an actual schedule change; "the Tuesday date was incorrect" identifies the communication error. Preserve the exact time zone.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
change record
Record describing a proposed change and its review or approval status.consult the approved change record
change owner
The role responsible for a proposed or approved change.identify the change owner
change approval
Permission to carry out a specified change.verify change approval
maintenance notice
A communication of planned service work and impact.correct a maintenance notice
maintenance window
The authorized time period for planned work.confirm the maintenance window
release
A version or package made available under a process.coordinate a software release
deployment
Introduction of a release or configuration into an environment.distinguish release from deployment
rollback plan
An approved route for returning to an earlier state.review the rollback plan
implementation plan
The defined plan for carrying out a change.confirm the implementation plan
validation
Checks that a change meets its intended requirements.record post-change validation
stakeholder
A person or group with an interest in the outcome.identify affected stakeholders
recipient list
The designated audience for a message.reconcile the recipient list
correction
A statement replacing inaccurate information.issue an explicit correction
supersede
Replace an earlier record or statement as controlling.supersede the earlier notice
source of truth
The agreed authoritative record for a particular fact.name the source of truth
acknowledgment
Confirmation of receipt or understanding.request acknowledgment
dependency
Work or a condition another action relies on.identify a scheduling dependency
change freeze
A defined restriction on changes during a period.check the change-freeze policy
emergency change
A change handled under an authorized urgent process.follow the emergency-change route
problem record
A record tracking an underlying cause or recurring issue.open a problem record
known error
A recognized problem with documented diagnostic context or handling.document a known error
post-implementation review
Review of a change's outcome after implementation.conduct a post-implementation review
action owner
The person accountable for an agreed follow-up.name the action owner
due date
The agreed date by which an action is expected.confirm the action due date

Acknowledgment confirms receipt or understanding; it does not approve the change. A corrected notice supersedes the inaccurate communication, while the approved change record remains the authority for the work.

C Notice the language

A correction needs an explicit replacement

Sending another message without identifying the error can leave two apparent sources of truth. Acknowledge the mistake, state the approved details, say which message is replaced, and address consequences already caused. Avoid implying that the authorized schedule changed when only the notice was wrong.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Own the error
The Tuesday date in my earlier notice was incorrect.
State the approved fact
The approved window is Thursday, 8 to 9 p.m., local branch time.
Replace the old record
This correction supersedes the earlier maintenance notice.
Avoid a false history
The approved window has not changed; the notice was wrong.
Acknowledge impact
I understand your team rescheduled an export based on that message.
Request confirmation
Please confirm that the corrected date has reached the affected team.
Name the authority
The approved change record remains the source of truth.
Close with an owner
I will send the correction and contact the team that already changed its schedule.
Check the audience
Use the original recipient list and any known forwarded recipients.
Preserve the zone
Include local branch time in the subject and body where appropriate.
Distinguish receipt
Acknowledging this correction does not provide change approval.
Check dependencies
Which other scheduled work depended on the incorrect date?
Avoid an assumption
We should not assume a second email has been read.
Record the consequence
Note the export rescheduling as a known impact of the error.
Separate follow-up
Review how the mismatch occurred after the immediate correction is sent.
Make action testable
Assign an owner to verify the notice against the approved record before sending.
6 language and precision notes
Was incorrect versus changed
The correct response corrects a statement; the second describes a change in the underlying fact.
Supersedes
This verb states which communication takes precedence when two versions circulate.
Apology with action
"I am sorry for the confusion; here is the corrected window" pairs accountability with useful information.
Local time
Name the relevant location or time zone rather than assuming every recipient reads the same clock.
Please confirm that
This requests confirmation of a specific fact, not a vague reply such as "looks good."
Passive alternatives
"An error was made" hides the responsible action; "I sent the wrong date" is clearer when that responsibility is known.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which subject line matches the facts?

Answer and explanation

D. Correction: Thursday maintenance window; Tuesday date was incorrect

The correct response corrects the message without inventing a change to the approved schedule or completed work.

2. Which sentence identifies the controlling communication?

Answer and explanation

B. This correction supersedes the earlier notice.

Supersedes explicitly replaces the inaccurate notice and reduces competing interpretations.

3. What does receipt acknowledgment establish?

Answer and explanation

A. That the recipient received or understood the correction

Receipt or understanding is narrower than approval or completion of downstream actions.

4. Which follow-up is concrete and accountable?

Answer and explanation

B. Name an owner to verify future notices against the approved record.

A named verification action can be performed and checked; vague intentions and deleted history do not create that control.

Check explanations in the book, page 97 ↗

E Conversations

Correct the maintenance date before users act on it

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Mina: Service communications analyst / Owen: Change coordinator

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • correction
  • audit trail
  • action owner
  • Acknowledgment
  • source of truth
  • impact
  • maintenance window
  • supersedes
  • recipient list
  • dependency
  1. Mina
    I found an error in yesterday's maintenance notice. I wrote Tuesday from eight to nine, but the approved record says Thursday during that same hour.
  2. Owen
    We need an explicit 1 before more teams act on it. Has any work started, or is this still only a communication mismatch?
  3. Mina
    The maintenance has not begun. One team moved its invoice export because of my notice, so there is already a scheduling consequence even without a service interruption.
  4. Owen
    Include that known 2. We should correct the date and contact that team directly rather than assume the general email will resolve everything.
  5. Mina
    Should I title the message maintenance moved to Thursday? That sounds concise, but it could imply Tuesday was once the authorized schedule, which it was not.
  6. Owen
    Exactly. Say the Tuesday date was incorrect and state the approved 3. The authorization did not change; our message failed to match it.
  7. Mina
    I will include Thursday, eight to nine p.m., local branch time. I also need to say that the new message replaces the earlier notice in case both circulate.
  8. Owen
    Use 4. That makes the relationship explicit. Put the correct date prominently so recipients do not have to compare paragraphs to find it.
  9. Mina
    I have the original mailing list. Some managers may have forwarded the message, so the first list may not include everyone who now has the wrong date.
  10. Owen
    Reconcile the 5 and ask managers to pass on the correction where needed. Do not claim that sending one email proves everyone received the right information.
  11. Mina
    For the team that moved the export, I will apologize and ask whether the corrected window conflicts with their revised schedule. I cannot change that schedule on their behalf.
  12. Owen
    Good. Identify the 6 and let its owner confirm the adjustment. Acknowledge the disruption without promising an action you do not own.
  13. Mina
    Do we need fresh change approval because I am issuing a corrected notice, or does the existing approved record remain valid for Thursday's work?
  14. Owen
    The approved record remains the 7 for this case. We are correcting its communication, not requesting a different window or scope.
  15. Mina
    I'll call the export team as well. I need to know they've seen the correct time and whether it creates another scheduling problem.
  16. Owen
    Precisely. 8 and authorization are different. The team's reply can confirm that they know the right time and identify remaining scheduling conflicts.
  17. Mina
    After the urgent correction, we should review how the wrong date entered the notice. I copied an earlier planning note instead of checking the approved record.
  18. Owen
    Record a concrete follow-up with an 9: verify the date, scope, and time zone against the approved record before each notice is sent.
  19. Mina
    I'll send it now and call the export team next. I'll let you know this afternoon if anyone still has a conflict with Thursday.
  20. Owen
    Thank you. Keep the correction and its 10. Clear replacement, accurate timing, and accountable follow-up matter more than making the original mistake disappear.

Answers and explanations
  1. correction Turn 2. A correction replaces inaccurate information; another unexplained message could leave both dates appearing valid.
  2. impact Turn 4. Impact includes the team's changed work schedule; the absence of an outage does not mean the mistaken notice had no consequences.
  3. maintenance window Turn 6. The maintenance window is the authorized work period, which remains Thursday from eight to nine in this case.
  4. supersedes Turn 8. Supersedes states that the corrected communication replaces the earlier one as the version recipients should use.
  5. recipient list Turn 10. The recipient list identifies the message audience; known forwarding may require further communication beyond the original addressees.
  6. dependency Turn 12. A dependency is scheduled work affected by another condition; the export plan may now depend on confirmation of the maintenance timing.
  7. source of truth Turn 14. The source of truth is the agreed authoritative record; the communication correction does not itself alter or renew the underlying approval.
  8. Acknowledgment Turn 16. Acknowledgment confirms receipt or understanding, not permission to carry out the change or proof that every resulting action is finished.
  9. action owner Turn 18. An action owner is accountable for completing the agreed improvement, making follow-up more specific than a general request to be careful.
  10. audit trail Turn 20. The audit trail preserves what was sent, corrected, and confirmed, allowing later review without hiding the original communication error.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Mina: I found an error in yesterday's maintenance notice. I wrote Tuesday from eight to nine, but the approved record says Thursday during that same hour.
  2. Owen: We need an explicit correction before more teams act on it. Has any work started, or is this still only a communication mismatch?
  3. Mina: The maintenance has not begun. One team moved its invoice export because of my notice, so there is already a scheduling consequence even without a service interruption.
  4. Owen: Include that known impact. We should correct the date and contact that team directly rather than assume the general email will resolve everything.
  5. Mina: Should I title the message maintenance moved to Thursday? That sounds concise, but it could imply Tuesday was once the authorized schedule, which it was not.
  6. Owen: Exactly. Say the Tuesday date was incorrect and state the approved maintenance window. The authorization did not change; our message failed to match it.
  7. Mina: I will include Thursday, eight to nine p.m., local branch time. I also need to say that the new message replaces the earlier notice in case both circulate.
  8. Owen: Use supersedes. That makes the relationship explicit. Put the correct date prominently so recipients do not have to compare paragraphs to find it.
  9. Mina: I have the original mailing list. Some managers may have forwarded the message, so the first list may not include everyone who now has the wrong date.
  10. Owen: Reconcile the recipient list and ask managers to pass on the correction where needed. Do not claim that sending one email proves everyone received the right information.
  11. Mina: For the team that moved the export, I will apologize and ask whether the corrected window conflicts with their revised schedule. I cannot change that schedule on their behalf.
  12. Owen: Good. Identify the dependency and let its owner confirm the adjustment. Acknowledge the disruption without promising an action you do not own.
  13. Mina: Do we need fresh change approval because I am issuing a corrected notice, or does the existing approved record remain valid for Thursday's work?
  14. Owen: The approved record remains the source of truth for this case. We are correcting its communication, not requesting a different window or scope.
  15. Mina: I'll call the export team as well. I need to know they've seen the correct time and whether it creates another scheduling problem.
  16. Owen: Precisely. Acknowledgment and authorization are different. The team's reply can confirm that they know the right time and identify remaining scheduling conflicts.
  17. Mina: After the urgent correction, we should review how the wrong date entered the notice. I copied an earlier planning note instead of checking the approved record.
  18. Owen: Record a concrete follow-up with an action owner: verify the date, scope, and time zone against the approved record before each notice is sent.
  19. Mina: I'll send it now and call the export team next. I'll let you know this afternoon if anyone still has a conflict with Thursday.
  20. Owen: Thank you. Keep the correction and its audit trail. Clear replacement, accurate timing, and accountable follow-up matter more than making the original mistake disappear.
Dialogue in the book, page 64 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Correcting Tuesday to Thursday

The lesson's change notice incorrectly announced Tuesday; the approved maintenance window is Thursday and users already received the notice.

  1. Change coordinator: I sent the wrong maintenance date. The notice said Tuesday, but the approved window is Thursday.
  2. Service owner: Users already received it. Please make the correction clear rather than silently replacing the notice.
  3. Change coordinator: I'll acknowledge the error and identify Thursday as the approved maintenance day.
  4. Service owner: Will you also change the technical runbook to match the mistaken Tuesday announcement?
  5. Change coordinator: No. The communication was wrong; it doesn't authorize changing the approved schedule.
  6. Service owner: Good. We should not create a second change while trying to repair the first message.
  7. Change coordinator: I'll use the original distribution route and label the new notice as a correction.
  8. Service owner: Please avoid inventing a new start time if the correction only concerns the day.
  9. Change coordinator: Agreed. I'll preserve the approved details and make the date correction explicit.
  10. Service owner: Once sent, confirm distribution so the support team can answer questions consistently.
Rollback available is not rollback completed

A separate fictional release: an update said rollback was complete, but the runbook only described an available rollback option; no execution is recorded.

  1. Release manager: My status update said rollback was complete. The record actually shows only that a rollback option exists.
  2. On-call engineer: Then users may believe we restored the previous version when no execution is recorded.
  3. Release manager: I'll correct that directly: rollback is available, not confirmed as performed.
  4. On-call engineer: Can you also avoid stating that the service is restored on that basis?
  5. Release manager: Yes. Recovery status needs its own evidence; the runbook isn't proof of an outcome.
  6. On-call engineer: What should the next update say about the release decision?
  7. Release manager: That the current status is being verified and any rollback decision will follow the authorized process.
  8. On-call engineer: I'll check the execution record and service evidence without assuming the earlier message was accurate.
  9. Release manager: I'll acknowledge my incorrect wording through the same incident channel.
  10. On-call engineer: Then we'll issue a verified status rather than let a possible action become a reported event.
Closing the incident did not close the problem

A separate fictional service incident is resolved, but the underlying-cause investigation remains open; an update mistakenly says all work is finished.

  1. Problem manager: The update says all work is finished, but the underlying-cause investigation is still open.
  2. Service coordinator: I confused restoring service with completing the problem investigation. I'll correct that wording.
  3. Problem manager: Please retain the confirmed restoration rather than making it sound as though service failed again.
  4. Service coordinator: So the corrected message should separate the resolved incident from the open problem record?
  5. Problem manager: Exactly. Users need an accurate service status and visibility of the remaining investigation.
  6. Service coordinator: Should I name a root cause to make the update feel more complete?
  7. Problem manager: No. An unresolved cause should remain unresolved in the message, however inconvenient that sounds.
  8. Service coordinator: I'll explain that investigation continues and link the problem record for authorized readers.
  9. Problem manager: I'll supply the next verified finding when available, rather than promise a conclusion today.
  10. Service coordinator: Thanks. I'll send an explicit correction and preserve both statuses accurately.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Correct the maintenance date before users act on it
  1. Check the answers. Read Thursday, 8 to 9 p.m., local branch time as the approved window; Tuesday was a notice error.
  2. Switch roles. Send the correction to the original recipients and address the known export rescheduling; do not describe a newly changed approval.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A release notice lists the wrong version

A notice says version 4.2 will be deployed. The approved record specifies version 4.1. Deployment has not begun, and the approved version has not changed.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Communicator: "The 4.2 reference was ___; the approved version is 4.1."

Answer and explanation

C. incorrect

The error is in the notice; the approved version remains 4.1.

2. Coordinator: "State that the correction ___ the earlier notice."

Answer and explanation

B. supersedes

Supersedes tells readers which communication replaces the inaccurate version.

3. Communicator: "The approved record remains the source of ___."

Answer and explanation

C. truth

The controlled record, not either informal assumption, governs the authorized version.

4. Coordinator: "Ask recipients to acknowledge the correction, not to provide new ___."

Answer and explanation

A. approval

Receipt of a corrected notice is distinct from authorizing a different deployment.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Original fictional case: A maintenance notice sent to users says Tuesday from 8 to 9 p.m. The approved change record says Thursday from 8 to 9 p.m., local branch time. The work has not begun. Language workshop: Correct the maintenance date before users act on it
Communication goal: Replace an inaccurate notice, acknowledge impact, and confirm the controlling record.
Language guidance: A mistaken notice is not automatically a changed authorization. "The date has moved" would imply an actual schedule change; "the Tuesday date was incorrect" identifies the communication error. Preserve the exact time zone.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

08Platform, DevOps, Observability, and Kubernetes ConversationsNormal CPU does not prove checkout is healthy+

Your goal

Challenge a narrow health claim and coordinate investigation around user-visible evidence.

This lesson in the book, page 67 ↗

A Read the situation

Normal CPU does not prove checkout is healthy

The checkout service's dashboard shows normal server CPU usage. Five users report slow checkout during the last half hour. Request-duration and database-wait data have not yet been reviewed. Pat owns the application; Suri coordinates the platform team. The service runs in Kubernetes. There is no confirmed cause or approved production change. Both teams can review approved telemetry and agree on the next investigation owner.

Pat: Application engineer / Suri: Platform engineer

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which metric has been reviewed?

Answer and explanation

D. Server CPU usage

The briefing confirms only the CPU panel; the other evidence has not been reviewed.

2. What do the five reports establish?

Answer and explanation

D. Five users experienced slow checkout in the reported period.

The reports establish a user-visible symptom, not a cause or the total affected population.

3. What is authorized now?

Answer and explanation

B. Review approved telemetry and coordinate investigation ownership

The case authorizes evidence review and coordination, not unapproved changes or unsupported resolution.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A Kubernetes readiness check reports a configured condition, not universal correctness of every user transaction. CPU utilization is one metric; waiting on another component can produce slow responses without high CPU on the observed server.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
platform
Shared technical services supporting applications.coordinate with the platform team
DevOps
Collaborative practices linking software delivery and operations.clarify DevOps responsibilities
Kubernetes
A platform for managing containerized workloads and services.inspect Kubernetes workload status
cluster
A group of machines or resources managed together.review cluster-level signals
node
A machine participating in a Kubernetes cluster.inspect node resource usage
pod
A Kubernetes unit containing one or more containers.check pod readiness
container
An isolated execution environment packaged with application dependencies.inspect container logs
deployment
A Kubernetes resource managing a desired set of replicated pods.review the deployment status
service
In Kubernetes, an abstraction exposing a group of endpoints.inspect the service endpoints
ingress
A resource defining external HTTP or HTTPS routing to services.review ingress routing
readiness
Whether an instance is ready to receive service traffic.check readiness status
liveness
Whether a running instance should be considered alive under its check.distinguish liveness from readiness
replica
A copy of an application instance maintained for a workload.compare replica behavior
resource request
A declared resource amount used in scheduling decisions.review resource requests
resource limit
A configured ceiling governing resource use.inspect resource limits
CPU
Central processing unit; processing capacity measured by the panel.inspect CPU utilization
observability
Ability to understand system behavior from emitted signals.improve service observability
metric
A numerical measurement collected over time.correlate service metrics
log
A recorded event or message from a system.review application logs
trace
A connected record of a request across components.follow a distributed trace
span
A timed operation within a distributed trace.identify a slow span
saturation
Capacity pressure at the most constrained resource, often reflected in waits.investigate resource saturation
database wait
Time spent awaiting a database resource or response.measure database wait time
user journey
The sequence of actions producing a user-visible outcome.monitor the checkout user journey

In Kubernetes, Deployment and Service have specific resource meanings; in general IT discussion the same words have broader uses. Name the resource or user-facing function to avoid talking past another team.

C Notice the language

A healthy component is not a healthy journey

A dashboard can be correct and incomplete. Normal CPU describes one resource measure, not the entire user transaction. Invite application and platform evidence together instead of using a green panel to dismiss user reports or assign blame.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Validate both observations
The CPU panel is normal, and users are reporting slow checkout.
Challenge the inference
That metric alone does not establish service health.
Ask about the journey
Which part of checkout is slow from the user's perspective?
Request timing
Can we compare request durations during the reported half hour?
Avoid blame
We have not isolated the application or platform as the cause.
Follow the request
Trace the transaction across the components rather than stopping at one panel.
Name missing signals
Database wait and complete request duration remain unchecked.
Assign the next review
Application will review request traces; platform will check the corresponding resource signals.
Clarify a resource
By service, do you mean the user-facing checkout or the Kubernetes Service?
Limit a health check
Readiness passes for its configured test; that is not a full checkout result.
Check the time range
Are both dashboards showing the same interval and time zone?
Separate mitigation
A proposed restart is not yet a diagnosed remedy.
Preserve authorization
Any production change still needs the approved route.
Report measured impact
We have five reports; the full affected population is not yet known.
Coordinate ownership
Who will combine the two teams' findings into the next update?
Close precisely
We will keep the issue open until user-visible behavior is assessed.
6 language and precision notes
And instead of but
"CPU is normal and checkout is slow" allows two compatible observations without dismissing either.
Does not establish
This challenges the strength of an inference without claiming that the metric itself is wrong.
By service
A clarification question identifies whether a word has a platform-specific or business meaning.
During
Tie evidence to the reported period rather than comparing unrelated dashboard windows.
Not yet
This preserves pending diagnosis and avoids treating an untested remedy as confirmed.
Who will
Assign one accountable coordinator rather than leaving ownership implicit across teams.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which statement combines the evidence accurately?

Answer and explanation

B. CPU is normal on the observed panel; checkout slowness still requires investigation.

A normal resource measure can coexist with slow transactions; the cause remains unconfirmed.

2. Which question resolves the meaning of service in this discussion?

Answer and explanation

B. By service, do you mean checkout or the Kubernetes Service resource?

Asking which meaning of service is intended separates the user-facing function from a named platform resource. The other questions concern freshness, ownership and time alignment.

3. What could a distributed trace help identify?

Answer and explanation

B. Which recorded operation contributes to the request delay

A trace records operations and timing for the traced request. Sampling limits and investigation are still needed before wider scope or causal conclusions.

4. Which next step fits the stated authority?

Answer and explanation

A. Review approved telemetry and coordinate findings before proposing a production change.

The case permits investigation and coordination, while changes and outcome promises need further evidence and approval.

Check explanations in the book, page 99 ↗

E Conversations

Normal CPU does not prove checkout is healthy

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Pat: Application engineer / Suri: Platform engineer

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • metric
  • change
  • database wait
  • handoff
  • user journey
  • observability
  • saturation
  • span
  • trace
  • readiness
  1. Pat
    Five users reported slow checkout in the last half hour. The platform dashboard shows normal CPU, so one colleague says the service must be healthy.
  2. Suri
    The panel can be correct without supporting that conclusion. CPU is one 1, and we need evidence about the user-visible transaction rather than dismissing the reports.
  3. Pat
    I agree. I have not reviewed the request durations yet. The reports say checkout feels slow, but they do not identify whether payment or order confirmation is taking longer.
  4. Suri
    Start by clarifying the 2. We can then compare request timing during the reported period with the corresponding platform signals.
  5. Pat
    When you say the service is ready, do you mean checkout has been tested or that the Kubernetes readiness checks are passing for its pods?
  6. Suri
    I mean the configured 3 checks pass. That is useful evidence about those checks, not proof that every checkout completes promptly.
  7. Pat
    Thanks for separating that. I can review the application trace for a reported transaction if we can match it to a request identifier and time.
  8. Suri
    A distributed 4 should help us follow the path. Please preserve the exact interval and time zone so our teams compare the same events.
  9. Pat
    If the slow operation is a database call, would normal application CPU be consistent with that? People seem to expect every slowdown to make CPU high.
  10. Suri
    It could be consistent with waiting, but we have not checked 5 yet. Describe that as a hypothesis until the timing evidence actually supports it.
  11. Pat
    Then I'll start with the slowest recorded operation. If the trace has a gap, I'll flag it rather than guess what happened there.
  12. Suri
    Good. Inspect the relevant 6, and avoid assuming that an uninstrumented interval contains no work. Missing telemetry is a limitation, not a zero-duration finding.
  13. Pat
    Should we restart the pods while the investigation continues? A colleague suggested it because that helped with a different issue last month, but this cause is unknown.
  14. Suri
    A restart would be a production 7. It needs the approved route and a reasoned proposal. We should not turn the previous incident's remedy into this incident's diagnosis.
  15. Pat
    I'll take the request path. What can your team check at the same time? I'd rather split the work than have us both checking everything.
  16. Suri
    We will examine corresponding resource use, workload status, and relevant 8 signals under our approved access. You can focus on application timing and the exact checkout steps.
  17. Pat
    Who will combine the findings for the next update? Separate team messages might sound contradictory if one says resources are normal and another says customers are waiting.
  18. Suri
    I will coordinate the 9. We can report both observations, explain their scope, and identify the next owner without declaring either team at fault.
  19. Pat
    Then the issue stays open. We have five reports, not a verified total of affected users, and no confirmed cause or approved remedy at this point.
  20. Suri
    Exactly. Better 10 means connecting the signals to the user outcome. We will report what the evidence establishes and what still needs investigation, even when the dashboard remains green.

Answers and explanations
  1. metric Turn 2. A metric measures a specified property; normal CPU does not establish the performance of the complete checkout transaction.
  2. user journey Turn 4. The user journey names the sequence producing the customer's outcome; a symptom should be located within that sequence.
  3. readiness Turn 6. Readiness reports whether an instance meets its configured traffic-acceptance check; it is not a complete test of every checkout behavior.
  4. trace Turn 8. A trace connects recorded operations across components for a request, helping locate where time is spent.
  5. database wait Turn 10. Database wait is time awaiting database work or resources; it is a plausible source of delay, not yet a finding here.
  6. span Turn 12. A span records a timed operation within the trace; it can show a contribution to delay without proving every unrecorded cause.
  7. change Turn 14. A production change requires the approved decision route; success on a previous unrelated issue does not authorize or establish this remedy.
  8. saturation Turn 16. Saturation concerns pressure on constrained capacity; waiting and resource signals complement request traces without assuming a normal CPU panel covers every resource.
  9. handoff Turn 18. A handoff combines the evidence and next responsibility so separate observations are not mistaken for conflicting conclusions.
  10. observability Turn 20. Observability is the ability to understand system behavior from its signals; useful interpretation connects those signals to user-visible outcomes.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Pat: Five users reported slow checkout in the last half hour. The platform dashboard shows normal CPU, so one colleague says the service must be healthy.
  2. Suri: The panel can be correct without supporting that conclusion. CPU is one metric, and we need evidence about the user-visible transaction rather than dismissing the reports.
  3. Pat: I agree. I have not reviewed the request durations yet. The reports say checkout feels slow, but they do not identify whether payment or order confirmation is taking longer.
  4. Suri: Start by clarifying the user journey. We can then compare request timing during the reported period with the corresponding platform signals.
  5. Pat: When you say the service is ready, do you mean checkout has been tested or that the Kubernetes readiness checks are passing for its pods?
  6. Suri: I mean the configured readiness checks pass. That is useful evidence about those checks, not proof that every checkout completes promptly.
  7. Pat: Thanks for separating that. I can review the application trace for a reported transaction if we can match it to a request identifier and time.
  8. Suri: A distributed trace should help us follow the path. Please preserve the exact interval and time zone so our teams compare the same events.
  9. Pat: If the slow operation is a database call, would normal application CPU be consistent with that? People seem to expect every slowdown to make CPU high.
  10. Suri: It could be consistent with waiting, but we have not checked database wait yet. Describe that as a hypothesis until the timing evidence actually supports it.
  11. Pat: Then I'll start with the slowest recorded operation. If the trace has a gap, I'll flag it rather than guess what happened there.
  12. Suri: Good. Inspect the relevant span, and avoid assuming that an uninstrumented interval contains no work. Missing telemetry is a limitation, not a zero-duration finding.
  13. Pat: Should we restart the pods while the investigation continues? A colleague suggested it because that helped with a different issue last month, but this cause is unknown.
  14. Suri: A restart would be a production change. It needs the approved route and a reasoned proposal. We should not turn the previous incident's remedy into this incident's diagnosis.
  15. Pat: I'll take the request path. What can your team check at the same time? I'd rather split the work than have us both checking everything.

Dialogue in the book, page 73 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Normal CPU does not measure checkout speed

The lesson's service shows normal server CPU while users report slow checkout.

  1. Product manager: The server CPU graph is normal. Doesn't that prove checkout is healthy?
  2. Site reliability engineer: It measures processor use, not the entire experience of completing a checkout.
  3. Product manager: Then the metric and the user reports aren't necessarily contradicting each other?
  4. Site reliability engineer: Correct. A delay elsewhere in the request path may not appear as high CPU.
  5. Product manager: Would a trace help explain that request path in plain terms?
  6. Site reliability engineer: Yes. A trace follows stages of a request; a log records particular events.
  7. Product manager: So we need checkout timing evidence, rather than another claim based on the CPU chart.
  8. Site reliability engineer: Exactly. We should inspect relevant metrics, logs, and traces before identifying a cause.
  9. Product manager: I'll stop calling the dashboard proof that users are mistaken.
  10. Site reliability engineer: I'll investigate the reported experience and distinguish confirmed timing from possible explanations.
A running pod is not a successful purchase

A separate fictional Kubernetes service: its pods are running, but no customer-journey test has been reviewed.

  1. Platform engineer: The dashboard says the pods are running. The release coordinator wants to call the service verified.
  2. Service owner: Running describes the workload's state, not whether a customer can complete a purchase.
  3. Platform engineer: Could you explain that distinction for the release meeting without platform terminology?
  4. Service owner: The software has started, but we haven't checked that the intended task works from beginning to end.
  5. Platform engineer: Then a normal infrastructure metric can't replace the customer-journey test we haven't reviewed.
  6. Service owner: Right. We need evidence at the level of the claim we're making.
  7. Platform engineer: I'll bring the workload status and mark the purchase verification as pending.
  8. Service owner: Please include any relevant trace when the test runs so we can locate a failure.
  9. Platform engineer: And if the test fails, we'll use logs for specific events rather than guess from pod status.
  10. Service owner: Exactly. Let's communicate two separate checks instead of compressing them into one green label.
An error budget is not permission to ignore users

A separate fictional reliability review: a service remains within its agreed monthly error budget, but users have reported a new failure pattern.

  1. Release lead: We still have error budget remaining. Can we ignore the new failure reports?
  2. Reliability engineer: No. The budget tracks allowed unreliability against our chosen objective; it doesn't make individual failures irrelevant.
  3. Release lead: So being within the monthly limit isn't the same as every workflow working correctly?
  4. Reliability engineer: Exactly. A monthly metric can hide a new pattern or a problem affecting a smaller group.
  5. Release lead: What evidence should we request before deciding whether another release can proceed?
  6. Reliability engineer: The affected workflow, timing, and related logs or traces, alongside the current objective measurements.
  7. Release lead: I'll ask for that review without claiming the budget itself grants release approval.
  8. Reliability engineer: Good. The decision should consider the new evidence and our agreed release process.
  9. Release lead: I'll describe the remaining budget as context, not as a dismissal of the reports.
  10. Reliability engineer: Then we can assess the actual risk without confusing a metric with a decision.

A search application's readiness check passes, but an authorized test request times out. The check only tests a lightweight endpoint. The search backend has not yet been reviewed.

Answer and explanation

Answer and explanation

A. journey

The complete user journey includes work beyond the lightweight readiness endpoint.

Answer and explanation

D. trace

A request trace can connect operations and timings along the path of the failing search.

4. Reviewer: "Keep the backend explanation as a ___ until the evidence supports it."

Answer and explanation

C. hypothesis

The backend has not been reviewed, so its involvement remains a proposed explanation.

Lesson 08: Platform, DevOps, Observability, and Kubernetes Conversations
Original fictional case: The checkout service's dashboard shows normal server CPU usage. Five users report slow checkout during the last half hour. Request-duration and database-wait data have not yet been reviewed. Pat owns the application; Suri coordinates the platform team. The service runs in Kubernetes. There is no confirmed cause or approved production change. Both teams can review approved telemetry and agree on the next investigation owner.
Language workshop: Normal CPU does not prove checkout is healthy
Communication goal: Challenge a narrow health claim and coordinate investigation around user-visible evidence.
Language guidance: A Kubernetes readiness check reports a configured condition, not universal correctness of every user transaction. CPU utilization is one metric; waiting on another component can produce slow responses without high CPU on the observed server.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The CPU panel is normal, and users are reporting slow checkout. / That metric alone does not establish service health. / Which part of checkout is slow from the user's perspective? / Can we compare request durations during the reported half hour? / We have not isolated the application or platform as the cause. / Trace the transaction across the components rather than stopping at one panel. / Database wait and complete request duration remain unchecked. / Application will review request traces; platform will check the corresponding resource signals. / By service, do you mean the user-facing checkout or the Kubernetes Service? / Readiness passes for its configured test; that is not a full checkout result. / Are both dashboards showing the same interval and time zone? / A proposed restart is not yet a diagnosed remedy. / Any production change still needs the approved route. / We have five reports; the full affected population is not yet known. / Who will combine the two teams' findings into the next update? / We will keep the issue open until user-visible behavior is assessed.
Vocabulary: platform: Shared technical services supporting applications. / DevOps: Collaborative practices linking software delivery and operations. / Kubernetes: A platform for managing containerized workloads and services. / cluster: A group of machines or resources managed together. / node: A machine participating in a Kubernetes cluster. / pod: A Kubernetes unit containing one or more containers. / container: An isolated execution environment packaged with application dependencies. / deployment: A Kubernetes resource managing a desired set of replicated pods. / service: In Kubernetes, an abstraction exposing a group of endpoints. / ingress: A resource defining external HTTP or HTTPS routing to services. / readiness: Whether an instance is ready to receive service traffic. / liveness: Whether a running instance should be considered alive under its check. / replica: A copy of an application instance maintained for a workload. / resource request: A declared resource amount used in scheduling decisions. / resource limit: A configured ceiling governing resource use. / CPU: Central processing unit; processing capacity measured by the panel. / observability: Ability to understand system behavior from emitted signals. / metric: A numerical measurement collected over time. / log: A recorded event or message from a system. / trace: A connected record of a request across components. / span: A timed operation within a distributed trace. / saturation: Capacity pressure at the most constrained resource, often reflected in waits. / database wait: Time spent awaiting a database resource or response. / user journey: The sequence of actions producing a user-visible outcome.
Speaking task: Check the dialogue. Read normal CPU and five slow-checkout reports as compatible observations, not proof that either is false. Switch roles. Name the unreviewed request-duration and database-wait evidence and coordinate the application and platform findings. Complete the search exchange. Distinguish the lightweight readiness check from the timed-out search journey and keep the backend cause unconfirmed.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: General IT English
Professional audience: IT operations, service desk, infrastructure, cloud, endpoint, security, and platform teams
Scope: Fictional workplace language practice, not instructions for administering real systems or handling live incidents. Follow authorized procedures, access controls, and qualified security review. Use invented accounts and records in practice.
Lesson 08: Platform, DevOps, Observability, and Kubernetes Conversations
Original fictional case: The checkout service's dashboard shows normal server CPU usage. Five users report slow checkout during the last half hour. Request-duration and database-wait data have not yet been reviewed. Pat owns the application; Suri coordinates the platform team. The service runs in Kubernetes. There is no confirmed cause or approved production change. Both teams can review approved telemetry and agree on the next investigation owner.
Language workshop: Normal CPU does not prove checkout is healthy
Communication goal: Challenge a narrow health claim and coordinate investigation around user-visible evidence.
Language guidance: A Kubernetes readiness check reports a configured condition, not universal correctness of every user transaction. CPU utilization is one metric; waiting on another component can produce slow responses without high CPU on the observed server.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The CPU panel is normal, and users are reporting slow checkout. / That metric alone does not establish service health. / Which part of checkout is slow from the user's perspective? / Can we compare request durations during the reported half hour? / We have not isolated the application or platform as the cause. / Trace the transaction across the components rather than stopping at one panel. / Database wait and complete request duration remain unchecked. / Application will review request traces; platform will check the corresponding resource signals. / By service, do you mean the user-facing checkout or the Kubernetes Service? / Readiness passes for its configured test; that is not a full checkout result. / Are both dashboards showing the same interval and time zone? / A proposed restart is not yet a diagnosed remedy. / Any production change still needs the approved route. / We have five reports; the full affected population is not yet known. / Who will combine the two teams' findings into the next update? / We will keep the issue open until user-visible behavior is assessed.
Vocabulary: platform: Shared technical services supporting applications. / DevOps: Collaborative practices linking software delivery and operations. / Kubernetes: A platform for managing containerized workloads and services. / cluster: A group of machines or resources managed together. / node: A machine participating in a Kubernetes cluster. / pod: A Kubernetes unit containing one or more containers. / container: An isolated execution environment packaged with application dependencies. / deployment: A Kubernetes resource managing a desired set of replicated pods. / service: In Kubernetes, an abstraction exposing a group of endpoints. / ingress: A resource defining external HTTP or HTTPS routing to services. / readiness: Whether an instance is ready to receive service traffic. / liveness: Whether a running instance should be considered alive under its check. / replica: A copy of an application instance maintained for a workload. / resource request: A declared resource amount used in scheduling decisions. / resource limit: A configured ceiling governing resource use. / CPU: Central processing unit; processing capacity measured by the panel. / observability: Ability to understand system behavior from emitted signals. / metric: A numerical measurement collected over time. / log: A recorded event or message from a system. / trace: A connected record of a request across components. / span: A timed operation within a distributed trace. / saturation: Capacity pressure at the most constrained resource, often reflected in waits. / database wait: Time spent awaiting a database resource or response. / user journey: The sequence of actions producing a user-visible outcome.
Speaking task: Check the dialogue. Read normal CPU and five slow-checkout reports as compatible observations, not proof that either is false. Switch roles. Name the unreviewed request-duration and database-wait evidence and coordinate the application and platform findings. Complete the search exchange. Distinguish the lightweight readiness check from the timed-out search journey and keep the backend cause unconfirmed.
Partner: Pat: Application engineer / Suri: Platform engineer
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A search application's readiness check passes, but an authorized test request times out. The check only tests a lightweight endpoint. The search backend has not yet been reviewed.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Pat: Who will combine the findings for the next update? Separate team messages might sound contradictory if one says resources are normal and another says customers are waiting.
Suri: I will coordinate the handoff. We can report both observations, explain their scope, and identify the next owner without declaring either team at fault.
Pat: Then the issue stays open. We have five reports, not a verified total of affected users, and no confirmed cause or approved remedy at this point.
Suri: Exactly. Better observability means connecting the signals to the user outcome. We will report what the evidence establishes and what still needs investigation, even when the dashboard remains green.
END REFERENCE

More situations, different skills

Three additional workplace scenarios

The backup succeeded; the recovery exercise did not

Explain a recovery-test shortfall using distinct time and data-loss measures.

A fictional recovery exercise starts at 10 a.m. The latest usable backup represents 2 a.m. data. Service is restored at 1:20 p.m. The approved objectives are a four-hour recovery point objective and a two-hour recovery time objective.

Ravi: Systems administrator / Leah: Service owner

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • restore duration
  • dependencies
  • test record
  • recovery point
  • elapsed time
  • acceptance criteria
  1. Leah
    The backup dashboard is green. Can I mark the recovery exercise successful?
  2. Ravi
    Not against both objectives. We restored service, but the 1 was three hours and twenty minutes.
  3. Leah
    Our target is two hours. Where did the extra time go?
  4. Ravi
    Most of it went into restoring the data. I have separate timings for provisioning, restoration, and application checks.
  5. Leah
    And how current was the recovered data?
  6. Ravi
    The 2 was 2 a.m., eight hours before the exercise started.
  7. Leah
    So we also missed the four-hour data-loss objective.
  8. Ravi
    Yes. The job completed, but the available recovery point was older than the business requirement allows.
  9. Leah
    Can we solve that by scheduling more backups?
  10. Ravi
    That may help, but first we should check why the later copy wasn't usable. I don't want to prescribe a fix before that review.
  11. Leah
    Agreed. What evidence should go in the report?
  12. Ravi
    The selected copy, its data timestamp, the 3, and the application validation results.
  13. Leah
    Were the recovered records actually checked, or did we stop when the service started?
  14. Ravi
    We checked the agreed sample and the totals for that backup. The checks passed within that defined scope.
  15. Leah
    Then the summary needs both the successful restoration and the missed objectives.
  16. Ravi
    I'll show them separately in the 4. No overall green status.
  17. Leah
    Who will propose the corrective work?
  18. Ravi
    I'll investigate the unusable copy. The storage team will review the duration. We'll bring you options with cost and 5.
  19. Leah
    Good. We'll choose a plan, then schedule another exercise.
  20. Ravi
    And define the 6 before rerunning it, including both objectives and the data checks.

Answers and explanations
  1. elapsed time Turn 2. Elapsed time is the interval actually taken, here from 10 a.m. to 1:20 p.m.
  2. recovery point Turn 6. Recovery point identifies the time represented by recovered data, not when restoration finished.
  3. restore duration Turn 12. Restore duration is the time spent recovering data, one part of the overall recovery time.
  4. test record Turn 16. Test record preserves the conditions, observations, and results of the recovery exercise.
  5. dependencies Turn 18. Dependencies are prerequisites or other work that the proposed corrective actions rely on.
  6. acceptance criteria Turn 20. Acceptance criteria specify the conditions the next exercise must meet before it can pass.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Leah: The backup dashboard is green. Can I mark the recovery exercise successful?
  2. Ravi: Not against both objectives. We restored service, but the elapsed time was three hours and twenty minutes.
  3. Leah: Our target is two hours. Where did the extra time go?
  4. Ravi: Most of it went into restoring the data. I have separate timings for provisioning, restoration, and application checks.
  5. Leah: And how current was the recovered data?
  6. Ravi: The recovery point was 2 a.m., eight hours before the exercise started.
  7. Leah: So we also missed the four-hour data-loss objective.
  8. Ravi: Yes. The job completed, but the available recovery point was older than the business requirement allows.
  9. Leah: Can we solve that by scheduling more backups?
  10. Ravi: That may help, but first we should check why the later copy wasn't usable. I don't want to prescribe a fix before that review.
  11. Leah: Agreed. What evidence should go in the report?
  12. Ravi: The selected copy, its data timestamp, the restore duration, and the application validation results.
  13. Leah: Were the recovered records actually checked, or did we stop when the service started?
  14. Ravi: We checked the agreed sample and the totals for that backup. The checks passed within that defined scope.
  15. Leah: Then the summary needs both the successful restoration and the missed objectives.
  16. Ravi: I'll show them separately in the test record. No overall green status.
  17. Leah: Who will propose the corrective work?
  18. Ravi: I'll investigate the unusable copy. The storage team will review the duration. We'll bring you options with cost and dependencies.
  19. Leah: Good. We'll choose a plan, then schedule another exercise.
  20. Ravi: And define the acceptance criteria before rerunning it, including both objectives and the data checks.
A weekly status report confuses backup and recovery

A manager wants to report that nightly backups prove the application can recover within two hours. No timed recovery test has established that claim.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Manager: Can I describe two-hour recovery as ___?

Answer and explanation

D. verified

Verified means supported by completed checks, which this claim currently lacks.

2. Administrator: Not yet. We have backup-job results, not a timed ___ test.

Answer and explanation

A. recovery

Recovery tests whether the service and data can be restored as required.

3. Manager: I'll label two hours as the agreed ___ instead.

Answer and explanation

A. objective

Objective describes a required target rather than an already measured result.

4. Administrator: Good. The exercise will provide the missing ___ evidence.

Answer and explanation

B. performance

Performance evidence shows what the recovery process actually achieved under test conditions.

Check the answers, then read both exchanges aloud. Swap roles on the second reading.
